Nowhere smooth critical points of polyconvex functionals in arbitrary dimension
Katarzyna Mazowiecka, Armin Schikorra

Abstract
. For any M , n ≥ 2 M,n\geq 2 and any open set Ω ⊂ ℝ n \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} we find a smooth, strongly polyconvex function F : ℝ M × n → ℝ F\colon\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R} and a Lipschitz map u : ℝ n → ℝ M u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M} that is a weak local minimizer of the energy ∫ Ω F ⁡ ( D ​ u ) . \int_{\Omega}F(Du). but with nowhere continuous partial derivatives. This extends celebrated results by Müller–S̆verák [ 12 ] and Székelyhidi [ 14 ] to higher dimensions.



1.
Introduction

Hilbert’s 19th problem, resolved by De Giorgi
[
3
]
and Nash
[
13
]
, asserts that maps
u\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
that locally minimize a functional
I(u)\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}F(Du)
with uniformly convex integrand
F
are actually smooth. This sort of result does not hold for systems, i.e. when considering vector-valued
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
[
4
,
7
,
11
]
. Evans in
[
6
]
and Kristensen–Taheri in
[
10
]
, showed that
partial
regularity results for
strong local minimizers
are still possible for systems
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
if
F
is strongly quasiconvex.

In striking contrast, Müller-S̆verák
[
12
]
developed convex integration techniques to construct a strongly quasiconvex map
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\to\mathbb{R}
and a Lipschitz but nowhere
C^{1}
-solution to the Euler–Lagrange equation of the corresponding energy
I(u)
. Shortly after, Székelyhidi
[
14
]
constructed strongly
polyconvex
examples
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\to\mathbb{R}
and Lipschitz but nowhere
C^{1}
maps
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
which are, by
[
10
]
, weak local minimizers of the corresponding energy.

In this work we extend Székelyhidi’s construction from the two-dimensional setting
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\to\mathbb{R}
and
u\colon\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
into strongly polyconvex
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
u\colon\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
, where the dimensions
M,n\geq 2
are arbitrary. A function
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
is called polyconvex if the map

\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\ni X\mapsto F(X)

can be written as a convex function of the
\ell\times\ell
subdeterminants of
X
,
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,\min\{n,M\}\}
. And
F
is strongly polyconvex if
F(X)-\gamma|X|^{2}
is polyconvex for some
\gamma>0
; cf.
Appendix

B
.

Precisely, our main result reads as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Take any
n,M\geq 2
. There exists a
C^{2}
-smooth, strongly polyconvex function
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
u\in{\rm Lip\,}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M})
such that
u
is a weak local minimizer of
u\mapsto\int_{{B}^{n}}F(Du)
, but
u
is nowhere continuously differentiable. More precisely,
\partial_{\alpha}u
is discontinuous in any open set
\Omega\subset{B}^{n}
and any
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

The notion of weak local minimizer is taken from
[
10
]
and in our setup the weak local minimizing property comes from the fact that
u
solves the Euler–Lagrange equations and
D^{2}F
is non-degenerate at a.e. point of
\nabla u(x)
.

Let us remark that a simple extension by zero of Székelyhidi’s
[
14
]
example does not lead to a
strongly
polyconvex function. Instead we need to adapt each step in his construction. The main subtlety that is when we work in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
the operation
{\rm div}
and
{\rm curl\,}
are not “simply a rotation” of each other — leading to necessary adaptations of the notion of “rank
1
”-connected sets, which in turn requires larger
T_{\aleph}
-configurations,
\aleph\gg 1
.

We also record that as a corollary of the proof
Theorem

1.1
we have

Theorem 1.2
.

Take any
n,M\geq 2
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be any bounded, open, convex set. There exists a
C^{2}
-smooth, strongly polyconvex function
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
and two different
u_{1},u_{2}\in{\rm Lip\,}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{M})
such that

•

u_{1}
and
u_{2}
both satisfy the Euler–Lagrange equation of the functional
I(u)=\int_{{B}^{n}}F(Du)
;

•

u_{1}=u_{2}
on
\partial\Omega
.

Outline:
Since we work in arbitrary dimension
n\geq 2
, we will work with differential forms, actually tensor products of differential forms. The basic definitions and the reformulation of the Euler–Lagrange equation of
I
to a differential inclusion
du\in K_{F}
is performed in
Section

2
.

Since we work with differential forms, the notion of “Rank one”-matrices needs to be replaced by what we call
\mathscr{R}
-connections, this leads to an adapted notion of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations. Definitions and dimensional analysis of those objects are presented in
Section

3
.

A given
F
which admits a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration, can be transformed into an
\tilde{F}
which allows for an abundance of transversal
T_{\aleph}
-configurations — at least when
\aleph
is large enough. This is discussed in
Section

4
and is one of the more subtle arguments in this work, although it still follows the basic philosophy of
[
14
]
.

Having a suitable replacement for “rank one” matrices, the notion of laminates, laminates of finite order change only slightly from
[
12
]
, we discuss this in
Section

5
. The way we add wiggles is then almost verbatim to
[
12
]
, see
Section

6
; so is the construction of the solution
u
which is described in
Section

7
. The only (small) subtlety is that
df\in L^{\infty}
does not imply Lipschitz bounds, but this is dealt with standard arguments from the Sobolev theory of differential forms
[
8
]
.

The construction of an
F
whose associated partial relation condition admits
some

T_{\aleph}
-configuration,
Section

8
, is more subtle: For
M=n=2
and
\aleph=5
this was proven with the help of a computer-algebra system by Székelyhidi in
[
14
]
. In principle this strategy seems to work for any
M,n\geq 2
as well – we checked this for
M=n=3
in
Appendix

C
. But in order to prove our result in any dimension
M,n\geq 2
we of course not rely on a computer-algebra system, especially since we need
T_{\aleph}
-configurations with
\aleph
very large. For this reason we present a method how to extend Székelyhidi’s
T_{5}
-configuration for
M=2
,
n=2
into a general, non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration in any dimension
M
and
n
, for any
\aleph
large.

With all these techniques in place, the proof of
Theorem

1.1
is then analogous to
[
14
]
, see
Section

9
.

Notation:

•

We will use
\aleph
for the
T_{\aleph}
-configuration and
\mathfrak{i}
,
\mathfrak{k}
,
\mathfrak{j}
,
\mathfrak{l}
for finite sequences, such as the components of a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration

•

Greek letters are reserved for domain indices
\alpha=1,\ldots n

•

latin letters are used for everything else.

Geometric Analysis and PDEs
, which received funding

2.
Notation and setup of the differential inclusion

As usual we denote the gradient of a map
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
as a matrix

Du=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{1}u^{1}&\ldots&\partial_{n}u^{1}\\
\partial_{1}u^{2}&\ldots&\partial_{n}u^{2}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{1}u^{M}&\ldots&\partial_{n}u^{M}\\
\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}.

The Euler–Lagrange system of the energy on some open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

I_{F}(u)\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}F(Du)\quad u\colon B^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}

for a Lagrangian
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

(2.1)

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\partial_{\alpha}\left((\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}F)(Du)\right)=0,\quad i=1,\ldots,M.

Since we are interested in particular in dimensions
n\geq 3
and the above is a divergence-type equation, the notion of differential forms simplifies notation, cf., e.g.,
[
5
]
. Let us also remark that some results regarding differential inclusions for differential forms were obtained in
[
2
]
.

For
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
we denote by
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}
the vector space of
k
-forms

\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}=\left\{\sum_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}\leq n}\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\,dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}:\quad\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}

spanned by the basis
(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots dx^{\alpha_{k}})_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}\leq n}
. It is completely equivalent to consider

\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}=\left\{\sum_{1\leq\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\leq n}\mu_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\,dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}:\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\in\mathbb{R}\\
\mu_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\beta,\gamma,\ldots,\alpha_{k}}=-\mu_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\gamma,\beta,\ldots,\alpha_{k}}\end{array}\right\},

since we henceforth agree on the convention that

dx^{\beta}\wedge dx^{\gamma}=-dx^{\gamma}\wedge dx^{\beta},\quad\text{in particular }dx^{\beta}\wedge dx^{\beta}=0.

For
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
M\geq 1
we denote by
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}
the vector space

\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}=\left\{\sum_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}\leq n}\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\,dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}\colon\quad\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\right\}.

In particular we will have “differential form operations” act component-wise with respect to the
\mathbb{R}^{M}
-component.

One of these operators is the Hodge star operator

\star\colon\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}\mathbb{R}^{n}.

It is defined as the unique linear operator with the following property. Fix
1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}\leq n
. Take
1\leq\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<\ldots<\beta_{n-k}\leq n
the complement of
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}
, i.e.,
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\}\cup\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-k}\}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Then we set

\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}\right)\coloneqq cdx^{\beta_{1}}\wedge dx^{\beta_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\beta_{n-k}},

where
c\in\{-1,1\}
is chosen so that

(2.2)

dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}\wedge\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}\right)=+dx^{1}\wedge dx^{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}.

For a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}
represented as a vector of
\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}
-vectors,

v=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}v^{1}\\
\vdots\\
v^{M}\\
\end{array}\right),\quad v^{i}\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}

we define

\star v=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\star v^{1}\\
\vdots\\
\star v^{M}\\
\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}\mathbb{R}^{n}.

An elementary, yet tedious, computation establishes that

\star\star v=(-1)^{k(n-k)}v\qquad\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-k}\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Another useful observation is

(2.3)

\star dx^{\alpha}=(-1)^{\alpha+1}dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha-1}\wedge dx^{\alpha+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}.

The other operation that is important to us is the differential

d\colon C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n})\to C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k+1}\mathbb{R}^{n}).

For
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
we say that a map
u\in C^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n})
if

u(x)=\sum_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}\leq n}\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}(x)\,dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}

and its coefficients
\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}(x)\in C^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M})
.

If
u\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n})
as above
we define the differential
du\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{k+1}\mathbb{R}^{n})
via

du(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{k}\leq n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\lambda_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}(x)\,dx^{j}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{k}}.

In particular any
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
can be identified as
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{0}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
Du\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
is then equivalent to considering
du\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Here is the point of all these algebraic considerations:

Using (
2.2
), the Euler–Lagrange system (
2.1
) written in the language of differential forms is

(2.4)

\begin{split}&\star d\star\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}F)(du)dx^{\alpha}\right)=0,\qquad i=1,\ldots,M.\end{split}

For (
2.4
) it is sufficient (on star-shaped domains: equivalent) to obtain the existence of
w^{i}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(2.5)

dw^{i}=\star\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}(\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}F)(du)dx^{\alpha}.

We thus see that
u\colon\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}
satisfies (
2.1
) if there exists a
w\colon\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}du\\
dw\end{array}\right)\in K_{F}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right),

where

(2.6)

K_{F}\coloneqq\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\colon\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}F(X)\star dx^{\alpha}\right)_{i=1}^{M}\end{array}\right)\right\}.

Observe that we can naturally identify
\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, so we will, equivalently, refer to

K_{F}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right).

However, this identification should be taken with care, since the Hodge star operator changes signs.

It is worth to compare this in the classical case
n=2
and
M=2
to the formulations in
[
12
,
14
,
15
]
: in that case we have
\star dx^{1}=dx^{2}
and
\star dx^{2}=-dx^{1}
. Moreover, we can identify
\mathbb{R}^{2}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
, so that we have

\begin{split}K_{F}\coloneqq&\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\\
\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\\
\end{array}\right)\colon\quad Y_{i1}=-\partial_{X_{i2}}F(X),\ Y_{i2}=\partial_{X_{i1}}F(X)\right\}\\
=&\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\\
\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\\
\end{array}\right)\colon\quad Y=-DF(X)J\right\}\\
\end{split}

where we denote

J=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-1\\
1&0\end{array}\right).

Thus, for
M=n=2
we see that we consider the same situation as in
[
12
,
14
,
15
]
.

3.
\mathscr{R}
–connections and
T_{\aleph}
-configurations

3.1.
\mathscr{R}
–connections

In our convex integration scheme we need to replace the notion of rank-1 connectedness to incorporate the linear algebra that the higher-dimensional case necessitates. For this, we replace the tensor product
q\otimes a
for
a\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

[
12
,
14
]
by an alternative tensor product. It turns out that the right notion is the following (the main reason for this particular choice is
Lemma

6.1
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
A,B\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
. We say that
A,B
are
\mathscr{R}
-connected if

A-B\in\mathscr{R},

where

(3.1)

\begin{split}\mathscr{R}&=\left\{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a\colon\quad\text{for some $b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and $a\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)$}\right\}\\
&\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

Again, in dimension
n=M=2
the set
\mathscr{R}
simply consists
\mathbb{R}^{4\times 2}
-matrices with rank
\leq 1
.

We want to rule out trivial movements in
\mathscr{R}
so we will denote

(3.2)

\mathscr{R}^{o}=\left\{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a\colon\quad\text{for some $b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}$ and $a\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)$}\right\}.

In colloquial terms one may refer to
\mathscr{R}
-matrices as “rank one”, and more generally one could say a matrix is “Rank
k
” if it can be represented as a sum of
k
elements of
\mathscr{R}
— but caution is needed: if
n>2
the set
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)
is
not

2M
-dimensional, but larger. On the other hand, if
n>2
the “tensor product”
b_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}\wedge a
has more cancellations than in the case
n=2
. We will discuss the proper representation and do a careful dimensional analysis below.

But first we record the some simple properties of
\mathscr{R}
:

Lemma 3.2
.

The set
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{R}^{o}
have the following properties:

(1)

\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{R}^{o}
are symmetric. If
A-B\in\mathscr{R}
then
B-A\in\mathscr{R}
.

(2)

\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{R}^{o}
are a cone, i.e., if
A-B\in\mathscr{R}
then
\lambda(A-B)\in\mathscr{R}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

The above formulation of
\mathscr{R}
is helpful for differential computations, for dimensional analysis it is less so. This is why we record the following identification:

Remark 3.3
.

The following is a linear bijection:

\mathfrak{T}:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)

\left(\mathfrak{T}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\omega^{\prime}\\
\omega^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\right)\right)_{i,\alpha}\coloneqq\left(\star\left(\omega^{\prime}\wedge\star dx^{\alpha}\right)\right)^{i}\quad i=1,\ldots,M,\ \alpha=1,\ldots,n

and

\left(\mathfrak{T}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\omega^{\prime}\\
\omega^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\right)\right)_{(M+i),\alpha}\coloneqq\left(\star\left(dx^{\alpha}\wedge\omega^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)^{i}\quad i=1,\ldots,M,\ \alpha=1,\ldots,n.

Consider the following set
\tilde{\mathscr{R}}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)

\tilde{\mathscr{R}}\coloneqq\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,b_{1}&\vec{u}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{i=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right):\text{ where }\begin{array}[]{l}b\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\ \vec{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{M},\\
\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta}=-\vec{v}_{\beta\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,n\end{array}\right\}

Then
\mathscr{R}
is equivalent to
\tilde{\mathscr{R}}
in the sense that we can pass from one set to the other by identifying

(3.3)

a=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{u}\\
\sum\limits_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\leq n}\vec{v}_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right).

Proof.

Observe that for
1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\leq n
we have

\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)=(-1)^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+1}dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{1}-1}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{1}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}-1}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}.

Thus, in view of (
2.3
),

dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)=(-1)^{\alpha_{2}}dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}-1}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}=-\star dx^{\alpha_{2}}

and

dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)=(-1)^{\alpha_{1}+1}dx^{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{\alpha_{1}-1}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{1}+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx^{n}=\star dx^{\alpha_{1}}.

Then, if we have (
3.3
),

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}\wedge a=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{u}\ \sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}\\
\sum\limits_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\leq n}\vec{v}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\left(b_{\alpha_{1}}\,\underbrace{dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)}_{=-\star dx^{\alpha_{2}}}+b_{\alpha_{2}}\,\underbrace{dx^{\alpha_{2}}\wedge\star\left(dx^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)}_{{=\star dx^{\alpha_{1}}}}\right)\end{array}\right).

Using antisymmetry we rewrite

(3.4)

\begin{split}\sum\limits_{1\leq\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}\leq n}&\vec{v}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\left(b_{\alpha_{1}}\,(-\star dx^{\alpha_{2}})+b_{\alpha_{2}}\,\star dx^{\alpha_{1}}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\left(b_{\alpha_{2}}\,\star dx^{\alpha_{1}}-b_{\alpha_{1}}\,\star dx^{\alpha_{2}}\right)\\
&=\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{\alpha,\beta}b_{\beta}\right)\,\star dx^{\alpha}.\end{split}

Thus,

\mathfrak{T}\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}\wedge a\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,b_{1}&\vec{u}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{i=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right).

∎

We also observe the following “quantitative connectivity” of
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
under movements in
\mathscr{R}
: We can connect any two “matrices” in
A,B\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
by
\mathscr{R}
-matrices of length roughly comparable to
|A-B|
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Fix
M,n\geq 2
. There exists a constant
\Lambda>1
and
N\geq 2
such that the following holds. For any

A,B\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

there are

(A_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{N}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that

•

A_{1}=A
and
A_{N}=B

•

A_{\mathfrak{i}+1}-A_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathscr{R}

•

|A_{\mathfrak{i}+1}-A_{\mathfrak{i}}|\leq\Lambda|A-B|
.

Proof.

In view of
Remark

3.3
it suffices to show that for any
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{2M\times n}
there exists
A_{\mathfrak{i}}
as above with
A_{\mathfrak{i}+1}-A_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\tilde{\mathscr{R}}
.

Let
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the unit vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then we can write

A-B=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}a_{\alpha}\otimes e_{\alpha}

with

|a_{\alpha}|\precsim|A-B|.

So in order to prove the claim, we may assume w.l.o.g.

A-B=a\otimes e_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{a}^{\prime}&0&\ldots&0\\
\vec{a}^{\prime\prime}&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{a}^{\prime}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vec{a}^{\prime\prime}&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right)

for some
a=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{a}^{\prime}\\
\vec{a}^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2M}
with
|\vec{a}^{\prime}|,|\vec{a}^{\prime\prime}|\precsim|A-B|
.

Set
\vec{u}^{1}\coloneqq\vec{a^{\prime}}^{1}
,
\vec{v}^{1}=0
,
\vec{b}^{1}=e_{1}
. Then the corresponding element
C^{1}\in\tilde{\mathscr{R}}
is

C^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}^{1}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{a}^{\prime}&0&\ldots&0\\
0&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right).

Set
\vec{u}^{2}\coloneqq 0
,
\vec{v}^{2}_{12}=\vec{a^{\prime\prime}}
, and
\vec{v}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}=0
for
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(1,2),(2,1)
, and lastly
\vec{b}^{2}=e_{2}
. Then the corresponding element
C^{2}\in\tilde{\mathscr{R}}
is

C^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vec{v}_{12}^{2}&\vec{v}_{22}^{2}&\vec{v}_{32}^{2}&\ldots&\vec{v}_{n2}^{2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&\ldots&0\\
\vec{a}^{\prime\prime}&0&\ldots&0\end{array}\right).

Thus,

C^{1}+C^{2}=B-A

and we can set

A_{1}=A,\ A_{2}=A+C_{1},\ A_{3}=B=A+C_{1}+C_{2}.

which implies the claim.
∎

The identification from
Remark

3.3
is also useful for the dimensional analysis:

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
b,\tilde{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|b|=|\tilde{b}|=1
, and
\vec{u},\vec{\tilde{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
not both zero, and
\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta}=-\vec{v}_{\beta\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,n
as well as
\vec{\tilde{v}}_{\alpha\beta}=-\vec{\tilde{v}}_{\beta\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,n
.

Then the following are equivalent

(1)

(b,\vec{u},\vec{v})
and
(\tilde{b},\vec{\tilde{u}},\vec{\tilde{v}})
represent the same object in
\mathscr{R}^{o}
, that is we have

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,b_{1}&\vec{u}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{1}&\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{1\alpha}\tilde{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{2\alpha}\,\tilde{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{n\alpha}\,\tilde{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

(2)

for
\lambda\in\{-1,1\}
we have

b=\lambda\tilde{b},\quad\vec{u}=\lambda\vec{\tilde{u}}

and if we take any orthonormal basis
(o_{1},\ldots,o_{n})
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
o_{1}=b
then we can write

(3.5)

\vec{v}-\lambda\vec{\tilde{v}}=\sum_{2\leq\alpha\leq\beta\leq n}\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}\left(o_{\alpha}\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes o_{\alpha}\right)

with
\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
for
2\leq\alpha\leq\beta\leq n
.

In particular,
\mathscr{R}^{o}
is locally a manifold of dimension

(3.6)

\dim\mathscr{R}^{o}=n(M+1)-1.

Proof.

Once the equivalence is proven the dimension (
3.6
) is clear: the degrees of freedom for
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
|b|=1
are
n-1
. The degrees of freedom for
\vec{u}
are
M
. The degrees of freedom for
w_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
for
2\leq\alpha\leq\beta\leq n
is
M
times the dimension of
\mathfrak{so}(n-1)
which is
\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}
. In view of (
3.5
) the latter is the dimension of the kernel of the map
(b,\vec{u},\vec{v})\mapsto\mathscr{R}^{o}
, so the degrees of freedom for
\vec{v}
are

M\left(\underbrace{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}_{\dim\mathfrak{so(n)}}-\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}\right)=M(n-1).

Adding up the degrees of freedom gives (
3.6
).

It remains to establish the equivalence.

(
2
)
\Rightarrow
(
1
)
is relatively obvious: Assuming (2) we clearly have

\vec{u}\,b_{\beta}=\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{\beta}\quad\beta\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Moreover since
o_{\alpha}\perp b
for
\alpha\geq 2
, we have

\sum_{2\leq\alpha\leq\beta\leq n}\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}\left(o_{\alpha}\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes o_{\alpha}\right)b=0.

Thus, from (
3.5
) we obtain

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{\gamma\alpha}\tilde{b}_{\alpha}=\lambda^{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{\gamma\alpha}b_{\alpha}+0.

This establishes (
1
).

(
1
)
\Rightarrow
(
2
)
We assume

(3.7)

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,b_{1}&\vec{u}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{1}&\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\tilde{b}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{1\alpha}\tilde{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{i=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{2\alpha}\,\tilde{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{n\alpha}\,\tilde{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

The first entry of the above equation is equivalent to

\vec{u}\otimes b=\vec{\tilde{u}}\otimes\tilde{b}.

Since by assumption
|b|=|\tilde{b}|=1
, this in turn is equivalent to
b=\pm\tilde{b}
and
\vec{u}=\pm\vec{\tilde{u}}
.

So in order to prove the equivalence we may w.l.o.g. assume
b=\tilde{b}
,
\vec{u}=\pm\vec{\tilde{u}}
and that

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\tilde{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

That is

(3.8)

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\vec{v}_{\beta\alpha}-\vec{\tilde{v}}_{\beta\alpha}\right)b_{\alpha}=0\quad\text{$\beta\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$}.

Let
(o_{1},\ldots,o_{n})
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
o_{1}=b
. We can then write

\vec{v}-\vec{\tilde{v}}=\sum_{1\leq\alpha<\beta\leq n}\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}\left(o_{\alpha}\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes o_{\alpha}\right)

for some “coefficients”
\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
.
All we need to show is that (
3.8
) is equivalent to to
w_{1,\beta}=0
for any
\beta\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
. But (
3.8
) is simply the matrix product of
\vec{v}-\vec{\tilde{v}}
(componentwise in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
-vector considered as an
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
-matrix). That is (
3.8
) implies

\sum_{1\leq\alpha<\beta\leq n}\underbrace{\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}}_{\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\underbrace{\left(o_{\alpha}\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes o_{\alpha}\right)}_{\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\underbrace{b}_{=o_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}=0

Since
(o_{1},\ldots,o_{n})
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(and
\beta\geq 2
)

0=\sum_{1\leq\alpha<\beta\leq n}\vec{w}_{\alpha\beta}\left(o_{\alpha}\delta_{\beta 1}-o_{\beta}\delta_{\alpha 1}\right)=\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{1\beta}o_{\beta}.

Since
o_{2},\ldots,o_{n}
are linear independent vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, considering the above equation componentwise in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
, we see that
\vec{w}_{1\beta}=0
for
\beta\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
. We can conclude.

∎

Next we state a continuity result of the parametrization of elements in
\mathscr{R}^{o}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
C\in\mathscr{R}
. Then there exists
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|b|=1
and
a=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)
such that

C=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a

and the following holds.

For any
\tilde{C}\in\mathscr{R}
there exists
\tilde{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|\tilde{b}|=1
and
\tilde{a}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{a}^{\prime}\\
\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)
such that

\tilde{C}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\tilde{b}_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge\tilde{a},

and denoting by
|C-\tilde{C}|
any norm of
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)
we have

(3.9)

|a^{\prime\prime}-\tilde{a}^{\prime\prime}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}}\precsim|C-\tilde{C}|+|b-\tilde{b}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

and

(3.10)

|a^{\prime}-\tilde{a}^{\prime}|\precsim|C-\tilde{C}|

and there exists constant
\sigma>0
(depending only on the choice of norms, otherwise uniform)

(3.11)

\text{if $|C-\tilde{C}|\leq\sigma|a^{\prime}|$}\quad\text{then }|b-\tilde{b}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\precsim\frac{1}{|a^{\prime}|}|C-\tilde{C}|.

In particular, if
C\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
there exists a small
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(C)>0
for any
\tilde{C}\in\mathscr{R}
with
|C-\tilde{C}|<\varepsilon
then
\tilde{C}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
and we can choose a parametrization
\tilde{a},\tilde{b}
that depends on
\tilde{C}
in a locally Lipschitz way.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.5
any
C
can be identified with some
|b|=1
,
\vec{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta}=-\vec{v}_{\beta\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
such that

\vec{v}=\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(b\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes b\right),

for some
\vec{w}_{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\beta\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
. The values of
\vec{w}_{\beta}
depend on the choice of the orthonormal frame
(o_{2},\ldots,o_{n})
that span
b^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
: if we adapt the orthonormal frame, i.e. for some
O\in O(n-1)
we set

\overline{o}_{\beta}\coloneqq\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}O_{\beta-1,\gamma-1}o_{\gamma}

we simply have

(3.12)

\vec{v}=\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{\overline{w}}_{\beta}\left(b\otimes\overline{o}_{\beta}-\overline{o}_{\beta}\otimes b\right),

where

\vec{\overline{w}}_{\beta}=\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}O_{\gamma-1,\beta-1}\vec{w}_{\gamma}.

We compute, for each
\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

\begin{split}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{\gamma\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\\
=&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(b\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes b\right)\right)_{\gamma\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\\
=&\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\left(b\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes b\right)\right)_{\gamma\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\\
=&\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(\left(b\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes b\right)b\right)_{\gamma}\\
=&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(o_{\beta}\right)_{\gamma}\\
\end{split}

That is,
C
is represented by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}&b\otimes\vec{u}\\
-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(o_{\beta}\right)_{1}&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(o_{\beta}\right)_{2}&\ldots&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(o_{\beta}\right)_{n}\end{array}\right)

Similarly, any
\tilde{C}\in\mathscr{R}
can be represented by

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}&\tilde{b}\otimes\tilde{\vec{u}}\\
-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{1}&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{2}&\ldots&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{n}\end{array}\right)

Adapting
(o_{2},\ldots,o_{n})
and
(\tilde{o}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{o}_{n})
, but without changing
b,\tilde{b},\vec{u},\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta}
we can ensure that

(3.13)

{\rm span\,}\{b,o_{2}\}={\rm span\,}\{\tilde{b},\tilde{o}_{2}\}\quad\text{ and }o_{\beta}\in\{\pm\tilde{o}_{\beta}\}\subset{\rm span\,}\{b,o_{2}\}^{\perp}\text{ for $\beta=3,\ldots,n$}

Indeed, if
\tilde{b}
is parallel to
b
we simply choose
\tilde{o}_{2}=o_{2}
. If
\tilde{b}
is not parallel to
b
we use the Gram-Schmidt-algorithm on
(\tilde{b},b)
to find
\tilde{o}_{2}
such that (
3.13
) holds.

The Hilbert-Schmidt scalar product of
\tilde{C}
and
C
becomes

\langle C,\tilde{C}\rangle=\langle b,\tilde{b}\rangle\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle+\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}\langle\vec{w}_{\beta},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\langle o_{\beta},\tilde{o}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

Thus

\begin{split}|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}=&|\vec{u}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}-2\langle b,\tilde{b}\rangle\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\\
&+|\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}+|\vec{w}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}-2\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}\langle\vec{w}_{\beta},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\,\langle o_{\beta},\tilde{o}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\\
=&|\vec{u}-\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|b-\tilde{b}|^{2}\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\\
&+\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}\left(|\vec{w}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\delta_{\beta\gamma}+|\vec{w}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\delta_{\beta\gamma}-2\langle\vec{w}_{\beta},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\langle o_{\beta},\tilde{o}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right)\\
=&|\vec{u}-\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|b-\tilde{b}|^{2}\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\\
&+|\vec{w}-\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}+\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}\langle\vec{w}_{\beta},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\left(2\delta_{\beta\gamma}-2\langle o_{\beta},\tilde{o}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right)\\
=&|\vec{u}-\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|b-\tilde{b}|^{2}\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\\
&+|\vec{w}-\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\\
&+\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\langle\vec{w}_{\beta},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\left(2-2\langle o_{\beta},\tilde{o}_{\beta}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right)\\
&+\sum_{\gamma=2}^{n}\sum_{\beta=2,\beta\neq\gamma}^{n}\langle\vec{w}_{\beta},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\left(-2\langle o_{\beta},\tilde{o}_{\gamma}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right).\end{split}

In view of (
3.13
) this simplifies to

\begin{split}|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}=&|\vec{u}-\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|b-\tilde{b}|^{2}\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\\
&+|\vec{w}-\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\\
&+\langle\vec{w}_{2},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\left|o_{2}-\tilde{o}_{2}\right|^{2}.\end{split}

Observe that for
\lambda,\mu\in\{-1,1\}
we have

\begin{split}&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}&\lambda\tilde{b}\otimes\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}\\
-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\mu\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\mu\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{1}&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\mu\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\mu\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{2}&\ldots&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\mu\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\mu\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{n}\end{array}\right)\\
&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}&\tilde{b}\otimes\tilde{\vec{u}}\\
-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{1}&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{2}&\ldots&-\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\tilde{\vec{w}}_{\beta}\left(\tilde{o}_{\beta}\right)_{n}\end{array}\right)\end{split}

Since (
3.13
) does not change if we replace
\tilde{b}
by
\lambda\tilde{b}
and
\tilde{o}_{\beta}
by
\lambda\tilde{o}_{\beta}
, we repeat the above computation and actually have for any
\lambda,\mu\in\{-1,1\}

\begin{split}|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}=&|\vec{u}-\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|b-\lambda\tilde{b}|^{2}\lambda\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\\
&+|\vec{w}-\mu\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\\
&+\mu\langle\vec{w}_{2},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\left|o_{2}-\mu\tilde{o}_{2}\right|^{2}\\
\end{split}

We choose
\lambda\in\{-1,1\}
so that
\lambda\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\geq 0
and
\mu\in\{-1,1\}
so that
\mu\langle\vec{w}_{2},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\geq 0
. Consequently,

(3.14)

\begin{split}|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}=&|\vec{u}-\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|b-\lambda\tilde{b}|^{2}\left|\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\right|\\
&+|\vec{w}-\mu\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\\
&+\left|\langle\vec{w}_{2},\tilde{\vec{w}}_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\right|\left|o_{2}-\tilde{o}_{2}\right|^{2}\\
\end{split}

A first consequence worth recording

(3.15)

\begin{split}|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}\geq&|\vec{u}-\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+|\vec{w}-\mu\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}\\
\end{split}

This implies (
3.10
) using the the representation (
3.3
).

From (
3.14
) we also find that if

|C-\tilde{C}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\vec{u}|\quad\text{then }|\vec{u}-\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\vec{u}|

and thus

\left|\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle\right|=\lambda\langle\vec{u},\tilde{\vec{u}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\left(|\vec{u}|^{2}+|\tilde{\vec{u}}|^{2}-|\vec{u}-\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}|^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{4}|\vec{u}|^{2}.

And thus

\begin{split}|C-\tilde{C}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\vec{u}|\quad\Rightarrow|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}\geq&|\vec{u}-\lambda\tilde{\vec{u}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}|b-\lambda\tilde{b}|^{2}|\vec{u}|^{2}\end{split}

This implies (
3.11
) using the the representation (
3.3
).

Similarly, if

|C-\tilde{C}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\vec{w_{2}}|\quad\text{then }\left|\langle\vec{w_{2}},\tilde{\vec{w_{2}}}\rangle\right|\geq\frac{1}{4}|\vec{w}_{2}|^{2}.

and thus

(3.16)

|C-\tilde{C}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\vec{w_{2}}|\quad\text{then }|C-\tilde{C}|^{2}\geq|\vec{w}-\mu\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M\times n-1}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}|\vec{w}_{2}|^{2}\left|o_{2}-\tilde{o}_{2}\right|^{2}\\

From the definitions of
\vec{v}
and
\vec{\tilde{v}}
, (
3.12
), also taking into account (
3.13
),

\begin{split}|\vec{v}-\vec{\tilde{v}}|\precsim&|\vec{w}-\mu\tilde{\vec{w}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}+|b-\lambda\tilde{b}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+|w_{2}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\,|o_{2}-\mu\tilde{o}_{2}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:Rcont:asdadfg2}}{\precsim}&|C-\tilde{C}|+|b-\lambda\tilde{b}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+|\vec{w_{2}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\,|o_{2}-\mu\tilde{o}_{2}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\\
\end{split}

This implies

|\vec{v}-\vec{\tilde{v}}|\precsim|C-\tilde{C}|+|b-\lambda\tilde{b}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}},

because either
|w_{2}|\leq 2|C-\tilde{C}|
or otherwise we can apply (
3.16
). This implies (
3.9
), using the representation (
3.3
).

For the last part of the claim, the continuous dependence of the parametrization of
\tilde{C}\approx C\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
we simply observe that up to the sign
a^{\prime}\neq 0
and
b
,
|b|=1
, are uniquely determined by
C
, by
Lemma

3.5
and (
3.3
), and thus (
3.11
) is applicable uniformly.
∎

From the continuous representation we obtain in particular

Lemma 3.7
.

\mathscr{R}
is a closed set in
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)

Proof.

Take any sequence
(C^{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathscr{R}
that converges in
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
.
Apply
Lemma

3.6
to
C^{1}
and
C^{\mathfrak{i}}
then we find a representation
We write

C^{\mathfrak{i}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a^{\mathfrak{i}}

with
b^{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
|b^{\mathfrak{i}}|=1
and

a^{\mathfrak{i}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a^{\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}}\\
a^{\mathfrak{i}^{\prime\prime}}\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right),

such that

\sup_{\mathfrak{i}}|a^{\mathfrak{i}}-a^{1}|\precsim\sup_{\mathfrak{i}}|C^{1}-C^{\mathfrak{i}}|+2<\infty.

Up to passing to a subsequence we thus can assume that
a^{\mathfrak{i}}
and
b^{\mathfrak{i}}
converge to some
a
and
b
, respectively, and we have

C\coloneqq\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a=\lim_{\mathfrak{i}\to\infty}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a^{\mathfrak{i}}=\lim_{\mathfrak{i}\to\infty}C^{\mathfrak{i}}.

∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\overline{C}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
.

Define the map

(3.17)

\mathfrak{P}:\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\to\mathscr{R}

by

\mathfrak{P}\left(\vec{u},\vec{v},b\right)\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{u}\,b_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}\\
\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{\alpha,\beta}b_{\beta}\right)\,\star dx^{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

for
\vec{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
(\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Then there exists an
\varepsilon>0
and a smooth manifold
\mathfrak{C}

\mathfrak{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)\times{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\to\mathscr{R}

which has

\dim\mathfrak{C}=Mn+n-1

and
\mathfrak{P}:\mathfrak{C}\to B(\overline{C},\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{R}
is a diffeomorphism.

Proof.

Fix
\overline{C}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
and take
Lemma

3.6
combined with
Lemma

3.5
some
\vec{\overline{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}
,
(\vec{\overline{v}}_{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
\overline{b}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
such that

\mathfrak{P}\left(\vec{\overline{u}},\vec{\overline{v}},\overline{b}\right)=C.

We define

\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)\times{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}

the set consisting of
\vec{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}
,
(\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\in\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
b\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
with the properties

•

|b-\overline{b}|<\frac{1}{2}
(this fixes the orientation of
b
)

•

\vec{v}
can be represented by for some
\vec{w}_{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\beta\in\{2,\ldots,n\}

\vec{v}=\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(b\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes b\right),

where
(b,o_{2},\ldots,o_{n})
denotes an arbitrary choice of orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which extends
b
.

Clearly
\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
is a smooth manifold of dimension
M+(n-1)+M(n-1)=Mn+n-1
(
M
-degrees of freedom for
\vec{u}
,
n-1
degrees of freedom of
b
, and then by the representation of
\vec{v}
we have
M(n-1)
-degrees of freedom for
\vec{w}
.

and
\mathfrak{P}:\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}\to\mathscr{R}^{o}
is a smooth operator. Also, if
\varepsilon
is chosen small enough, then by
Lemma

3.5
and
Lemma

3.6
any
C\in B(\overline{C},\varepsilon)
corresponds to exactly one tuple
(\vec{u},(\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n},b)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}
, indeed
Lemma

3.6
implies that
\mathfrak{P}
is bi-Lipschitz (Here we used
a^{\prime}\neq 0
).

Any smooth bi-Lipschitz map is locally a diffeomorphism onto its image,
Lemma

A.2
, so we can conclude.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix

(\overline{b},\vec{\overline{u}},\vec{\overline{v}})\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},

so that the corresponding

\overline{C}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}}\,\overline{b}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,\overline{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,{\overline{b}_{n}}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\in\mathscr{R}^{o}.

We also assume for some fixed orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

(\overline{b},o_{2},\ldots,o_{n})
that

\vec{\overline{v}}=\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{\overline{w}}_{\beta}\left(\overline{b}\otimes\overline{o}_{\beta}-\overline{o}_{\beta}\otimes\overline{b}\right),

Define the linear space

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{1}&\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right):\\
&(b,u,\vec{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\Big\}\\
\end{split}

Then

\dim\mathfrak{C}=n(M+1)-1

Proof.

This follows in the spirit of

Lemma

3.8
.
Fix an orthonormal basis
(\overline{b},o_{2},\ldots,o_{n})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then we may assume that

(3.18)

\vec{v}=\sum_{\beta=2}^{n}\vec{w}_{\beta}\left(b\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes b\right),

because all terms of the type
\left(o_{\alpha}\otimes o_{\beta}-o_{\beta}\otimes o_{\alpha}\right)
for
\alpha,\beta\neq 1
get cancelled.

If we write

b=\underbrace{b-\overline{b}\langle\overline{b},b\rangle}_{b^{T}}+\underbrace{\overline{b}\langle\overline{b},b\rangle}_{b^{\perp}}

We see that for
\vec{\tilde{u}}\coloneqq\vec{u}+\langle\overline{b},b\rangle

\begin{split}&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{1}&\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{T}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{T}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{T}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\overline{b}_{1}&\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\overline{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\tilde{u}}\,\overline{b}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
\end{split}

Thus

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{1}&\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u}\,\overline{b}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right):\\
&(b,u,\vec{v})\in T_{\overline{b}}{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:akjlchvxcvi} holds}\Big\}\\
\end{split}

This is an at most
(n-1)+M+(n-1)M
-dimensional space. And indeed,
from
Lemma

3.8
,
(\overline{b}+\varepsilon b,\overline{u}+\varepsilon u,\overline{v}+\varepsilon v)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}+o(\varepsilon)
, and we have that

(b,u,v)\mapsto\mathfrak{P}(\overline{b}+\varepsilon b,\overline{u}+\varepsilon u,\overline{v}+\varepsilon v)\in\mathscr{R}^{o}

is a local diffeomorphism, so the
\mathfrak{C}
is simply the tangent space, and thus has the same dimension as the manifold
\mathscr{R}^{o}
, which was computed in
Lemma

3.8
. We can conclude.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Fix

(\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}},\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}},\vec{\overline{v}^{\mathfrak{i}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\mathfrak{i}=1,2.

and set

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\Big\}\\
\end{split}

Also set

\overline{C}^{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

Assume that
(\overline{b}^{1},\overline{b}^{2})
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
(\overline{u}^{1},\overline{u}^{2})
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
.

Then

\overline{C}^{2}\not\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}

Proof.

Assume to the contrary that there exists
C^{1}\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}
with
C^{1}=\overline{C}^{2}
.
We take the first component of the equality which is

\overline{u}^{1}\otimes b^{1}+u^{1}\otimes\overline{b}^{1}=\overline{u}^{2}\otimes\overline{b}^{2}

There exists
P\in O(M)
and
O\in O(n)
such that
e_{1}=P\overline{u}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
e_{2}=P\overline{u}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
o_{1}=O\overline{b}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
o_{2}=O\overline{b}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{M}
and
o_{1},\ldots,o_{n}
are the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, respectively. Multiplying the equation from the left with
P
and from the right with
O
, setting
\tilde{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}=Ob^{\mathfrak{i}}
and
\tilde{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}=Pu
, we find

(3.19)

e_{1}\otimes\tilde{b}^{1}+\tilde{u}^{1}\otimes o_{1}=e_{2}\otimes o_{2}.

Multiplying with
e_{\ell}
and
o_{\alpha}
respectively we find

\delta_{1\ell}\langle\tilde{b}^{1},o_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\langle\tilde{u}^{1},e_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\delta_{1\alpha}=\delta_{2\ell}\delta_{2\alpha}\quad\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,M\},\ \alpha\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

Taking
\ell=1
and
\alpha=2,\ldots,n
we find that
\langle\tilde{b}^{1},o_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=0
, thus
\tilde{b}^{1}=\lambda o_{1}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Taking
\ell=2,\ldots,M
and
\alpha=1
we find that
\langle\tilde{u}^{1},e_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}=0
, thus
\tilde{u}^{1}=\mu e_{1}
for some
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Taking
\ell=1
and
\alpha=1
we find

\lambda+\mu=0.

Thus, (
3.19
) becomes

0=e_{2}\otimes o_{2}

a contradiction. We can conclude.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

For
M,n\geq 2
there exists
\lambda=\lambda(n,M)\in(1,2)
such that the following holds.
Fix

(\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}},\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}},\vec{\overline{v}^{\mathfrak{i}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\mathfrak{i}=1,2.

corresponding to some
\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
, as in
Lemma

3.9
. As in that lemma we set

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&(b,u,\vec{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\Big\}\\
\end{split}

Assume that
(\overline{b}^{1},\overline{b}^{2})
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
(\overline{u}^{1},\overline{u}^{2})
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
.

Then the set

K_{0}\coloneqq\left\{(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathfrak{C}^{1}\times\mathfrak{C}^{2}:\quad C_{1}-C_{2}=0\right\}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{2}

has dimension

\dim K_{0}\leq\lambda\left(n(M+1)-1\right)

Proof.

As in the proof of
Lemma

3.9
we can rewrite

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{l}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{l}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b^{\mathfrak{l}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{l}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{l}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{l}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{l}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{l}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{l}}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&(b,u,\vec{v})\in T_{\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{l}}}{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\ \text{with \eqref{eq:akjlchvxcvi}}\Big\}\\
\end{split}

and we may assume
\vec{v}^{\mathfrak{i}}
satisfies (
3.18
), i.e. is
(n-1)M
-dimensional,
b^{\mathfrak{i}}\in T_{\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}}{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
i.e. is
n-1
-dimensional.

Consider the equation

(3.20)

C^{1}-C^{2}=0

We consider the first components,

\begin{split}\vec{\overline{u}^{1}}\otimes b^{1}+\vec{u^{1}}\,\otimes\overline{b^{1}}=\vec{\overline{u}^{2}}\otimes b^{2}+\vec{u^{2}}\,\otimes\overline{b^{2}}\end{split}

There exists
P\in O(M)
and
O\in O(n)
such that
\vec{e}_{1}=P\vec{\overline{u}}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\vec{e}_{2}=P\vec{\overline{u}}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
o_{1}=O\overline{b}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
o_{2}=O\overline{b}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then we have

\begin{split}\vec{e}_{1}\otimes(Ob^{1})+P\vec{u^{1}}\,\otimes o_{1}=\vec{e}_{2}\otimes Ob^{2}+P\vec{u^{2}}\,\otimes o_{2}\end{split}

Multiply this with
e_{\ell}
and
o_{\alpha}
to obtain

(3.21)

\begin{split}\delta_{\ell 1}\langle(Ob^{1}),o_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\langle P\vec{u^{1}},\vec{e}_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\delta_{\alpha 1}=\delta_{\ell 2}\langle Ob^{2},o_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}+\langle P\vec{u^{2}},e_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\delta_{\alpha 2}\end{split}

Take
\alpha=1
and
\ell=1
, then we find

(3.22)

\langle P\vec{u^{1}},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}=-\langle(Ob^{1}),o_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

This equation restricts
u^{1}
in terms of
b^{1}
.
Set

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{1}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{1}}\,b^{1}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{1}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{1}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b^{1}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b^{1}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b^{1}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{1}}\,\overline{b^{1}}_{1}&\vec{u^{1}}\,\overline{b^{1}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{1}}\,\overline{b^{1}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{1}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b^{1}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{1}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b^{1}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{1}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b^{1}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&(b,u,\vec{v})\in T_{\overline{b}^{1}}{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\ \text{with \eqref{eq:akjlchvxcvi} and \eqref{eq:xclkxcvijx}}\Big\}\\
\end{split}

Then we see that

\dim\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{1}\leq(n-1)+(M-1)+(n-1)M,

and

\{(C^{1},C^{2}):C^{1}-C^{2}=0\}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}^{1}\times\mathfrak{C}^{2}.

By Cartesian coordinates, the dimension of the right-hand side space is at most

(n-1)+(M-1)+(n-1)M+(n-1)+M+(n-1)M=\underbrace{\frac{2(Mn+n-1)-1}{(Mn+n-1)}}_{=:\lambda<2}(Mn+n-1).

We can conclude.

∎

3.2.
T_{\aleph}
-configurations

We are now ready to define what we mean by a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration – it is essentially the same definition as in
[
12
]
– but we replace “rank-one connections” with
\mathscr{R}
-connections. Note, however, that the notion of non-degeneracy is relatively strong.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\aleph\in{\mathbb{N}}
. A
T_{\aleph}
-configuration consist of

•

a base point
P\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)

•

(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\subset\mathscr{R}
such that
\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{i}}=0

•

(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\in(1,\infty)

Given such a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration for
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
we set

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})\coloneqq P+C_{1}+C_{2}+\ldots C_{\mathfrak{k}-1}

and

(3.23)

Z_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})\coloneqq P+C_{1}+C_{2}+\ldots C_{\mathfrak{k}-1}+\kappa_{\mathfrak{k}}C_{\mathfrak{k}}.

We call a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration
non-degenerate
if all of the following hold in addition to the above assumptions.

(1)

C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
for all
\mathfrak{i}
,

(2)

if we write
C^{\mathfrak{i}}
as

C^{\mathfrak{i}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\mathfrak{i}=1,\ldots\aleph,

for
b^{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
|b^{\mathfrak{i}}|=1
, and

a^{\mathfrak{i}}\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)

then we have

(3.24)

{\rm span}\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{\aleph}\}=\mathbb{R}^{n}

(3)

and

(3.25)

\{b_{\mathfrak{i}},b_{\mathfrak{i}+1}\}\text{ linear independent}\quad\text{whenever }\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

(where we identify
\aleph+1
with
1
)

(4)

and

\{a_{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime},a_{\mathfrak{i}+1}^{\prime}\}\text{ linear independent}\quad\text{whenever }\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

(5)

and

(3.26)

Z_{\mathfrak{k}}-Z_{\mathfrak{l}}\not\in\mathscr{R}\quad\text{for all $\mathfrak{k}\neq\mathfrak{l}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}$}.

We call a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration
wild
for some
\beta
if, when we write

Z_{\mathfrak{k}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X_{\mathfrak{k}}\\
Y_{\mathfrak{k}}\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

and identify
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
with the canonical matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
, then

(3.27)

\left\{X_{\mathfrak{k}}e_{\beta}:\quad\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}

consists of at least two different vectors, where
e_{\beta}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the usual
\alpha
-th unit vector.

We call a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration
wild
if it is wild for each
\beta\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

We observe that necessarily
\aleph\geq n
for any non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration. Also our definition (
3.24
) is a somewhat strong generalization of the non-degeneracy for
T_{\aleph}
-configurations in
\mathbb{R}^{4\times 2}
which assumes that there are no rank-
1
connections between the points
\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
,
\mathfrak{k}=1,\ldots,n
. Our assumption (
3.24
) is needed to ensure that the collection of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations around a specific non-degenerate one form a smooth manifold.

The wildness (
3.27
) will be used to ensure that for our map
u
from
Theorem

1.1
the partial derivative
\partial_{\alpha}u
has high oscillation for each
\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Assume that
\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
is a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration.

Then there exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that if
\tilde{P},(\tilde{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}
is an (a priori possibly degenerate)
T_{\aleph}
-configuration which is
\varepsilon
-close to
P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}
, i.e. if

•

|P-\tilde{P}|\leq\varepsilon

•

|C^{\mathfrak{i}}-\tilde{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}|\leq\varepsilon
for all
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

•

|\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}-\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}}|\leq\varepsilon
for all
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

Then
\left(\tilde{P},(\tilde{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
is also non-degenerate.

Proof.

(1)

By the continuity result of
Lemma

3.6
, if
C\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
then for suitably close-by
\tilde{C}\in\mathscr{R}
we have
\tilde{C}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
.

(2)

Follows from the same continuity continuity result
Lemma

3.6

(3)

Follows from the same continuity continuity result
Lemma

3.6

(4)

Follows from the same continuity continuity result
Lemma

3.6

(5)

According to
Lemma

3.7

\mathscr{R}
is a closed set. Thus, if (
3.26
) holds for
\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
then it holds also for all
T_{\aleph}
-configuration which are suitably close.

∎

The following elementary, yet crucial observation about
T_{\aleph}
-configurations use only the definition of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations, and
Lemma

3.2
.

Remark 3.14
.

Assume that
(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
is a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration. As in (
3.23
) we set the base points

P_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}),\quad\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

and the end-points

Z_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\phi_{k}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}),\quad\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}.

For any
\mu\in(0,1]
the set
(P,(\mu C^{\mathfrak{i}})_{i=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
is still a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration, with

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(\mu C^{\mathfrak{i}})_{i=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})=P+\mu C_{1}+\ldots+\mu C_{\mathfrak{k}-1},

and

\begin{split}\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(\mu C^{\mathfrak{i}})_{i=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})=&P+\mu C_{1}+\ldots+\mu C^{\mathfrak{k}-1}+\kappa_{\mathfrak{k}}\mu C^{\mathfrak{k}}\\
=&(1-\mu)P+\mu Z_{\mathfrak{k}}\\
\end{split}

The latter implies in particular that if
(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
is a nondegenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration, so is
(P,(\mu C^{\mathfrak{i}})_{i=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
.

3.3.
Dimensional Analysis

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth, strongly polyconvex function. Then
K_{F}
as in (
2.6
)
is an
Mn
-dimensional manifold.

Moreover, at the point
Z=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)
we have the tangent space

T_{Z}K_{F}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{X}\\
D^{2}F(X)[\tilde{X}]\end{array}\right):\quad\tilde{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\right\}

Proof.

K_{F}
consists of

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that
Y=\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}F(X)\star dx^{\alpha}\right)_{i=1}^{M}
, which means that
K_{F}
can be identified with

\tilde{K}_{F}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}X\\
Y\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):\quad\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{i\alpha}=\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}F(X)\\
i=1,\ldots,M\text{ and }\alpha=1,\ldots,n\end{array}\right\}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)

Since
F
is strongly polyconvex
D^{2}F(X)
satisfies the strong Legendre-Hadamard condition and thus
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\ni Z\mapsto D^{2}F(X)Z
is invertible,
Lemma

B.5
. By the implicit function theorem we find that then
\tilde{K}_{F}
must be a
Mn
-dimensional manifold.
∎

For
\aleph\in{\mathbb{N}}
we denote the set

(3.28)

\mathcal{K}_{F}\coloneqq K_{F}\times\ldots K_{F}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}.

We also want to define a manifold within the “endpoints of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations”

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

i.e.

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\subset\left\{(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}:\quad\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{\mathfrak{k}}=\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})\\
\text{for some $T_{\aleph}$-configuration $P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}$}\end{array}\right\}

where we recall the definition of
T_{\aleph}
-configuration from
Definition

3.12
and in particular (
3.23
). A word of warning: we will – in a common abuse of notation – sometimes call
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
as the manifold of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations (instead of the more precise “set of endpoints of some specific
T_{\aleph}
-configurations”).

The precise choice of
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
, and the word “manifold” are from following important observation: around any fixed
(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})\in\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
derived from a
non-degenerate

T_{\aleph}
-configuration, we can find such a manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
whose dimension we can compute. For the case
M=n=2
this has been done in
[
12
, Section 4.2]
,
[
9
, Proposition 4.26]
and we extend the approach in
[
14
, Lemma 2]
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
be a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration.

Denote the
\mathfrak{k}
-th endpoint of the above
T_{\aleph}
-configuration as

\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}).

Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}
of
T_{\aleph}
-endpoints containing (
\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph}
).
This manifold is of dimension

\dim\left(\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap B_{\varepsilon}((\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph}))\right)={\aleph}\,n(M+1)

Moreover, for any tuple
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
there exists a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration
({P},({C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},({\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
such that

Z_{\mathfrak{i}}=\phi_{\mathfrak{i}}({P},({C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},({\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}),

and the dependency of
P,C_{\mathfrak{i}},\kappa
on
Z_{\mathfrak{i}}
is smooth.

In particular there exists a smooth map (with a slight but justifiable abuse of notation called again
\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}
)

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that for any tuple
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
if we take
({P},({C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},({\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})
from above
then

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(Z_{\mathfrak{i}})=P+C_{1}+\ldots+C_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Moreover, for each
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
,

(3.29)

D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}:T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\hat{=}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)

is surjective.

To prove
Proposition

3.16
, the main point is to properly parametrize
(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}
with the property
\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{i}}=0
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Set

\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}\coloneqq\left\{(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\subset\mathscr{R}:\quad\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{i}}=0\right\}

Assume that
(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\in\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}
are nondegenerate in the sense that

(1)

\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathscr{R}^{o}
for
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
,

(2)

if we write

\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge\overline{a}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\mathfrak{i}=1,\ldots\aleph,

for some

\overline{a}^{\mathfrak{i}}\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\setminus\{0\}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)

and
\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
|\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}|=1
, then we have

(3.30)

{\rm span}\{\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{\aleph}\}=\mathbb{R}^{n}

Then there exist
\varepsilon>0
such that the set

B((\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}),\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}

•

consists only of tuples
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})
which are non-degenerate in the above sense

•

can be smoothly parametrized via a smooth submanifold

\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}\subset\left(\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\aleph}

of dimension

(3.31)

\dim\mathfrak{C}_{N}={\aleph}\,(Mn+n-1)-2Mn

More precisely consider if we extend the map defined in (
3.17
) to tuples

\mathfrak{P}:\left(\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\aleph}\to\mathscr{R}^{\aleph}

via

\begin{split}&\mathfrak{P}\left((\vec{u}^{1},\ldots,\vec{u}^{\aleph}),(\vec{v}^{1},\ldots,\vec{v}^{\aleph}),(b^{1},\ldots,b^{\aleph})\right)\\
\coloneqq&\left(\mathfrak{P}\left((\vec{u}^{1},\vec{v}^{1},b^{1})\right),\mathfrak{P}\left((\vec{u}^{2},\vec{v}^{2},b^{2})\right),\ldots,\mathfrak{P}\left((\vec{u}^{\aleph},\vec{v}^{1},b^{\aleph})\right)\right)\end{split}

Then
\mathfrak{P}\Big|_{\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}}:\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}\to B((\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}),\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}
is a diffeomorphic parametrization.

Proof.

Fix

\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge\overline{a}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\mathfrak{i}=1,\ldots\aleph,

By continuity of parametrization,
Lemma

3.6
, any tuple
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})\in B((\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}),\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}
must be non-degenerate, if
\varepsilon
is small enough.

For the given nondegenerate
(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})\in\mathscr{R}^{\aleph}
we choose

(\vec{\overline{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{\overline{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{v}}^{\aleph}),(\overline{b}^{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\aleph})\in\mathfrak{C}^{\aleph}\subset\left(\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\aleph}\to\mathscr{R}^{\aleph},

where
\mathfrak{C}
is from
Lemma

3.8
(mind that this is a slight abuse of notation
\mathfrak{C}^{\aleph}=\mathfrak{C}_{1}\times\ldots\times\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}

where each
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}
is from
Lemma

3.8
for the specific
C_{\mathfrak{i}}
), and we have

\mathfrak{P}(\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}},\vec{\overline{v}}^{\mathfrak{i}},\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}})=\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}},

and we recall the non-degeneracy condition
{\rm span}\{\overline{b}^{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\aleph}\}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
(again, for
\varepsilon
small enough the
\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}
