are unique up to sign, as we evince from
Lemma

3.6
).

Next we define
\Phi:\mathscr{R}^{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

via

\Phi(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})\coloneqq\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{i}}.

We are going to show below that
\Phi

has full rank at
(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})
, i.e.

(3.32)

{\rm rank\,}D\Phi(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})=2Mn.

Once we have (
3.32
), the implicit function theorem implies the existence of a small
\varepsilon
such that
B((\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}),\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}
is a smooth manifold with dimension

\dim B((\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}),\varepsilon)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}={\aleph}\,\dim\mathscr{R}^{o}-2Mn,

where
\dim\mathscr{R}^{o}=Mn+n-1
was computed in
Lemma

3.8
. This establishes (
3.31
), inverting
\mathfrak{P}
for each tuple, by
Lemma

3.8
, we have found
\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}
.

It remains to establish (
3.32
). To show that
\Phi
has full rank, it suffices to show that
\Phi\circ\mathfrak{P}
has full rank at the point
(\vec{\overline{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{\overline{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{v}}^{\aleph}),(\overline{b}^{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\aleph})
.

For this we compute
D\left(\Phi\circ\mathfrak{P}\right)
at this point, and show it is surjective.
That is we need to show the following map is surjective

\begin{split}&(\vec{{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{v}}^{\aleph}),({b}^{1},\ldots,{b}^{\aleph})\mapsto\\
&\left(D(\Phi\circ\mathfrak{P})\left((\vec{\overline{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{\overline{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{v}}^{\aleph}),(\overline{b}^{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\aleph})\right)\right)[(\vec{{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{v}}^{\aleph}),({b}^{1},\ldots,{b}^{\aleph})]\end{split}

To simplify the notation slightly, we use the representation in
Remark

3.3
, and use as replacement for
\Phi\circ\mathfrak{P}
the map

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Phi}((\underbrace{b^{1}}_{\in\mathbb{R}^{n}},\underbrace{u^{1}}_{\in\mathbb{R}^{M}},\underbrace{v^{1}}_{\in{\mathfrak{s}o}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}}),(b^{2},u^{2},v^{2}),\ldots,(b^{\aleph},u^{\aleph},v^{\aleph}))\\
\coloneqq&\sum_{j=1}^{\aleph}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{j}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{j}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{j}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{1\alpha}b^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{2\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{n\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)\end{split}

Fix a matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{2M\times n}
written as

W=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U\\
V\\
\end{array}\right)

where
U\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
.

For surjectivity we need to find
\vec{u}^{\mathfrak{j}}
,
\vec{v}^{\mathfrak{j}}
and
b^{\mathfrak{j}}
such that

\begin{split}\left(D\tilde{\phi}\left((\vec{\overline{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{\overline{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{v}}^{\aleph}),(\overline{b}^{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\aleph})\right)\right)[(\vec{{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{v}}^{\aleph}),({b}^{1},\ldots,{b}^{\aleph})]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U\\
V\\
\end{array}\right).\end{split}

Our first choice is to set
b^{\mathfrak{j}}=0
for all
\mathfrak{j}
. Then we need to find
\vec{u}^{\mathfrak{j}}
and
\vec{v}^{\mathfrak{j}}
such that

\begin{split}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}U\\
V\\
\end{array}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{\aleph}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
\end{split}

It is easy to guess
u^{\mathfrak{j}}
: Since
{\rm span}\{\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{\aleph}\}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
we can write

U=u_{1}\otimes\overline{b}_{1}+u_{2}\otimes\overline{b}_{2}+\ldots+u_{\aleph}\otimes\overline{b}_{\aleph},

that is we have

U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

It remains to find
v^{1},\ldots,v^{\aleph}
such that

(3.33)

V=\sum_{j=1}^{\aleph}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

which we can do essentially in the same way as the construction for
U
, but with a little bit more linear algebra.

We define the pairing
L:\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
as follows: for
\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta}=-\vec{v}_{\beta\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we set

L_{(\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}}[b]\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{1\alpha}b_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{2\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v}_{n\alpha}\,b_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}

Our goal is to find
(\vec{v}^{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\in\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}
so that (
3.33
) holds, i.e.

(3.34)

V=\sum_{\mathfrak{j}=1}^{\aleph}L_{v^{\mathfrak{j}}}(\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{j}}).

Now we pick
\vec{\nu}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
1\leq\alpha,\beta,\gamma\leq n
with the following properties

•

V=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\nu^{2}}_{12}&-\vec{\nu^{1}}_{12}&\ldots&-\vec{\nu^{1}}_{1n}\end{array}\right)
,

•

v^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}=-v^{\gamma}_{\beta\alpha}

•

all other
v^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
are set to zero.

Then

\begin{split}V=&\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\nu^{\gamma}}_{1\gamma}&\vec{\nu^{\gamma}}_{2\gamma}&\ldots&\vec{\nu^{\gamma}}_{n\gamma}\end{array}\right)\\
=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&-\vec{\nu^{1}}_{12}&\ldots&-\vec{\nu^{1}}_{1n}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\nu^{2}}_{12}&0&\ldots&-\vec{\nu^{2}}_{2n}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\ldots\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\nu^{n}}_{1n}&\vec{\nu^{n}}_{2n}&\ldots&0\end{array}\right)\end{split}

Denote by
e_{\gamma}=(0,0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the
\gamma
-th unit vector. Then

L_{(\vec{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma})}[e_{\gamma}]=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\nu}^{\gamma}_{1\gamma}&\vec{\nu}^{\gamma}_{2\gamma}&\ldots&\vec{v}^{\gamma}_{n\gamma}\end{array}\right)

So, from our previous choice of
\vec{v}_{\alpha\beta}^{i}
we have

V=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}L_{(\vec{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma})}[e_{\gamma}]

Since
(\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{l}})_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}
spans all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have can find
\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\mathfrak{l}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
,

e_{\gamma}=\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}\gamma}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{l}}.

Then, by multilinearity,
we have

\begin{split}V=&\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}\gamma}L_{\left((\vec{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma})_{\alpha\beta}\right)}[\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{l}}]\\
=&\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}L_{\left((\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}\gamma}\vec{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma})_{\alpha\beta}\right)}[\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{l}}]\end{split}

Observe we still have
\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}\gamma}\vec{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=-\sum_{\gamma=1}^{n}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}\gamma}\vec{\nu}_{\beta\alpha}^{\gamma}
, so indeed we have (
3.34
).

So this implies if
(\overline{b}_{1},\ldots,\overline{b}_{\aleph})
spans all of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then for any

W\in\mathbb{R}^{2M\times n}

there exists some choice
(\vec{u}^{1},\ldots,\vec{u}^{\aleph})
,
(\vec{v}^{1},\ldots,\vec{v}^{\aleph})
such that

\left(D(\Phi\circ\mathfrak{P})\left((\vec{\overline{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{\overline{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{\overline{v}}^{\aleph}),(\overline{b}^{1},\ldots,\overline{b}^{\aleph})\right)\right)[(\vec{{u}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{u}}^{\aleph}),(\vec{{v}}^{1},\ldots,\vec{{v}}^{\aleph}),(0,\ldots,0)]=W.

And thus,
D\Phi(\overline{b},\overline{u},\overline{v})
is a surjective linear map, thus it has full rank, that is (
3.32
) is established.

We can conclude.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.16
.

Fix the
T_{\aleph}
-configuration
\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
.

Consider
\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}
from
Lemma

3.17

\Phi:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\times\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}\times(1,\infty)^{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph},

given by

(3.35)

\Phi\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)\coloneqq\left(\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})\right)_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}

We see that

D_{P}\Phi\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)[Q]=(Q,Q,\ldots,Q)

D_{\kappa_{\mathfrak{k}}}\Phi\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)[\kappa]=(0,\ldots,0,\kappa C_{\mathfrak{k}},0,\ldots,0)

and for
C\in T_{\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}}\mathscr{C}

D_{C_{\mathfrak{i}}}\Phi\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)[C]=(0,\ldots,0,\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}C,C,\ldots,C)

These seem to be all linear independent, so
D\Phi
has full rank. Observe that the domain has dimension

•

Choice of the base point
P\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
. Degrees of freedom:
2Mn
.

•

Dimension of
\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
is
{\aleph}\,(Mn+n-1)-2Mn
, by
Lemma

3.17
.

•

choice of
\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\aleph}
. Degrees of freedom:
{\aleph}
.

So the dimension of the domain of
\Phi
is
\aleph n(M+1)
and its map into an
2Mn\aleph
-dimensional space. By the implicit function space, for a suitably small
\varepsilon
we can set

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\coloneqq\left\{\Phi(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}):\ \begin{array}[]{l}|P-\overline{P}|<\varepsilon\\
\max_{\mathfrak{i}}|\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}}|<\varepsilon\\
|C_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}|<\varepsilon\end{array}\right\}

is a
\aleph n(M+1)
-manifold and
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism of a neighborhood of
(\overline{P},\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{n},\overline{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\kappa}_{\aleph})
onto
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
. In particular it is one-to-one onto its target, so for each
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
there exists exactly one
({P},{C}_{1},\ldots,{C}_{n},{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,{\kappa}_{\aleph})
in the neighborhood of
(\overline{P},\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{n},\overline{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\kappa}_{\aleph})
that
\Phi
maps into
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})
. So if we set

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\coloneqq P+C_{1}+\ldots+C_{\mathfrak{k}}

we have a well-defined smooth map on
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
.

Fix some
\mathfrak{k}
then for
|P-\overline{P}|<\varepsilon
,
\max_{\mathfrak{i}}|\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}}|<\varepsilon
,
|C_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}|
,

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}\circ\Phi(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})=P+C_{1}+\ldots+C_{\mathfrak{k}}

Already by the variations in
P
it is clear that (
3.29
) is satisfied.
∎

Lemma 3.18
.

For
\aleph\geq 1
let

(\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}},\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}},\vec{\overline{v}^{\mathfrak{i}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M},\quad\mathfrak{i}=1,\ldots,\aleph.

Set

\begin{split}\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\Big\{C\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right):C=&\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,b^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\\
&+\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{1}&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{u^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{v^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b^{\mathfrak{i}}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)\Big\}\\
\end{split}

Also set

\overline{C}^{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\vec{\overline{u}^{\mathfrak{i}}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}&\vec{\overline{u}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}&\ldots&\vec{\overline{u}}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}\\
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{1\alpha}\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{2\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}&\ldots&\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\vec{\overline{v}}_{n\alpha}\,\overline{b}^{\mathfrak{i}}_{\alpha}\end{array}\right)

Lastly fix
\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}}>0
for
\mathfrak{i}\in(1,\ldots,\aleph)
.

Assume that
\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}}
for
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
such that

(3.36)

\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{l}}=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\mathfrak{l}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}}C_{\mathfrak{l}}\quad\mathfrak{l}=1,\ldots,\aleph

and

(3.37)

\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{l}}=0.

Then
\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}=0
and
C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}}
for
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
.

Proof.

We argue by induction. If
\aleph=1
(
3.37
) implies
C_{1}=0
, and thus (
3.36
) implies
\kappa_{1}=0
.

Assume now the claim is proven for
\aleph-1
, and
\aleph\geq 2
.

We write down the first two equations (
3.36
)

(3.38)

\kappa_{1}\overline{C}_{1}=\overline{\kappa_{1}}C_{1}

\kappa_{2}\overline{C}_{2}=C_{1}+\overline{\kappa_{2}}C_{2}

Plugging the first equation into the second one we have

\kappa_{2}\overline{C}_{2}-\overline{\kappa_{2}}C_{2}=\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}\overline{C}_{1}.

Observe that
\overline{C}_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}^{2}
, which is a linear space, so we have

\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}\overline{C}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}^{2},

by
Lemma

3.10
this implies
\kappa_{1}=0
. By (
3.38
) we also have
C_{1}=0
. Now we can use the induction hypothesis.

∎

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
be a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration with endpoints
\overline{Z}=(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}
.
Take from from
Proposition

3.16

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
the manifold of
T_{\aleph}
-endpoints around
\overline{Z}
and
\pi_{1}:\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)
.

Then

\ker d\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

consists of tuples

(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

such that each
Z_{\mathfrak{l}}
,
\mathfrak{l}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
has the representation,

(3.39)

Z_{\mathfrak{l}}=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\mathfrak{l}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}}C_{\mathfrak{l}}+\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{l}}

where
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})\in T_{(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
and
(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\aleph})\in\mathbb{R}^{\aleph}
– Here we take the manifold
\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
around
(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})
from
Lemma

3.17
.

The representation in (
3.39
) is unique (in
C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}
and
\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\aleph}
) and we have

(3.40)

\dim\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})={\aleph}\,(Mn+n)-2Mn

More generally, if we set

\mathfrak{Z}\coloneqq\left\{(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}:\quad Z_{\mathfrak{l}}=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\mathfrak{l}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}}C_{\mathfrak{l}}\text{ for some $({C}_{1},\ldots,{C}_{\aleph})\in T_{(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}$}\right\}

\mathfrak{K}\coloneqq\left\{(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}:\quad Z_{\mathfrak{l}}=\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{l}}\text{ for some $(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\aleph})\in\mathbb{R}^{\aleph}$}\right\}

Then
\mathfrak{Z}\cap\mathfrak{K}=\{0\}
and

\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})=\mathfrak{K}+\mathfrak{Z}.

Proof.

We begin by computing
\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})
, that is we need to compute all

Z\coloneqq(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in T_{\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4\times 2}\right)^{\aleph}

such that

D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})[Z]=0.

From
Lemma

3.17
we take the manifold
\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
around
(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})
that consists of nondegenerate
\mathscr{R}
-tuples, parametrized from a submanifold

\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}\subset\left(\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\aleph}\to\mathscr{R}^{\aleph}

via the map
\mathfrak{P}\Big|_{\mathfrak{C}_{\aleph}}
,

\mathfrak{P}:\underbrace{\left(\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}}_{\vec{u}}\times\underbrace{\left(\mathfrak{so}(n)\otimes\mathbb{R}^{M}\right)^{\aleph}\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\aleph}}_{\vec{v}}\to\underbrace{\mathscr{R}^{\aleph}}_{b}

Then from
Proposition

3.16
we infer that
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
is parametrized by (
3.35
)

\Phi:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)\times\mathscr{C}_{\aleph}\times(1,\infty)^{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph},

\Phi\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)\coloneqq\left(\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})\right)_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}

Clearly

\pi_{1}\circ\Phi\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)=P

So for any
P\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)
,
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})\in T_{\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
,
\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\big|_{\varepsilon=0}\pi_{1}\circ\Phi\left(\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)+\varepsilon\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)\right)=P

That is

\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\big|_{\varepsilon=0}\Phi\left(\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)+\varepsilon\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)\right)\in\ker D\pi(\overline{Z})

if and only if
P=0
.

From the definition of
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{\aleph}
we find

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\big|_{\varepsilon=0}\Phi\left(\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)+\varepsilon\left(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)\right)=:(Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\end{split}

where

(3.41)

Z_{\mathfrak{l}}=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\mathfrak{l}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}}C_{\mathfrak{l}}+\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{l}}.

Since
\Phi
is a parametrization of
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
we have shown

(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})

if and only if (
3.41
) holds for some
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})\in T_{\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
,
\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
.

So consider the linear map

A:T_{\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

given by

A(\left(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}\right),\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{\aleph})=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})

where
Z_{\mathfrak{i}}
are given by (
3.41
). By
Lemma

3.18

A
is an invertible matrix. This establishes (
3.40
) since by
Lemma

3.17

\left(T_{\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph}}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}={\aleph}\,(Mn+n-1)-2Mn+n

The decomposition into
\mathfrak{K}
and
\mathfrak{Z}
follows from
Lemma

3.18
.
∎

The following is our version of
[
14
, Lemma 5]

Lemma 3.20
.

Fix
M,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n,M\geq 2
. There exist
\aleph_{0}=\aleph_{0}(n,M)
such that the following holds.
Assume that
\aleph\geq\aleph_{0}
and let
\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
be a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration with endpoints
\overline{Z}=(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}
.

Take from from
Proposition

3.16

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
the manifold of
T_{\aleph}
-endpoints around
\overline{Z}
and
\pi_{1}:\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)
.

Denote for
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq\aleph
the map

p_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:(\mathbb{R}^{Mn})^{\aleph}\to(\mathbb{R}^{Mn})^{\aleph}

which sets the
i_{j}
-components to be zero, i.e.

p_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\aleph})=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i_{1}-1},0,v_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,v_{i_{2}-1},0,v_{i_{2}+1},\ldots,\nu_{{i_{k}}-1},0,\nu_{{i_{k}}+1},\ldots,\nu_{\aleph}).

Then
\ker D\pi_{1}\subset T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}
contains an
Mn\aleph
-dimensional subspace
L
with the property that

\dim(L\cap{\rm im\,}p_{i_{1}\ldots,i_{k}})\leq Mn(\aleph-k)

for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
and
1\leq i_{1}<\ldots i_{k}\leq\aleph
.

Proof.

Fix
\overline{Z}=(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})\in\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{M\times n})^{\aleph}
.

We will show below that

(3.42)

\dim(\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\cap{\rm im\,}p_{i_{1}\ldots,i_{k}})\leq{\aleph}\,(Mn+n)-2Mn-Mnk

for all
1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq\aleph
and all
k\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
.

Assume for the moment that (
3.42
) is established, take an orthonormal basis

{\rm span\,}\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{K}\}=\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\cap{\rm im\,}p_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}},

where
K\leq\aleph(Mn+n)-2Mn-Mnk
. Recall from
Lemma

3.19
that

\dim\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})={\aleph}\,(Mn+n)-2Mn.

So we can extend the orthonormal system
\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{K}\}
above to an orthonormal system
\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{\aleph(Mn+n)-2Mn}\}
such that

{\rm span\,}\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{K},o_{K+1},\ldots,o_{\aleph(Mn+n)-2Mn}\}=\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z}).

Take

L\coloneqq{\rm span\,}\{o_{1},\ldots,o_{Mn\aleph-Mnk},o_{\aleph(Mn+n)-2Mn-Mnk+1},\ldots,o_{\aleph(Mn+n)-2Mn}\}.

Observe that this defines an
Mn\aleph
-dimensional space, if

Mn\aleph-Mnk<\aleph(Mn+n)-2Mn-Mnk+1,

i.e., if
\aleph\geq 2M
.

By definition of
L
,

\dim(L\cap{\rm im\,}p_{i_{1}\ldots,i_{k}})=Mn(\aleph-k).

That is, once we have established (
3.42
) we can conclude.

We need
to prove (
3.42
)
.

For
k=\aleph
this is easy, since
{\rm im\,}p_{1,\ldots,\aleph}=\{0\}
and
\aleph\geq 2M
.

So from now on we assume
k\leq\aleph-1
.

By
Lemma

3.19
any element
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\subset T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}
is then of the form

(3.43)

\begin{split}Z_{\mathfrak{l}}=&\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\mathfrak{l}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}}C_{\mathfrak{l}}+\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{l}}\end{split}

where
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph})\in T_{(\overline{C}_{1},\ldots,\overline{C}_{\aleph})}\mathcal{C}_{\aleph}
. Such a
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\cap{\rm im\,}p_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}
if and only if

(3.44)

\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{{i_{\ell}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell}}C_{{i_{\ell}}}=-\kappa_{{i_{\ell}}}\overline{C}_{i_{\ell}},\quad\ell=1,\ldots,k.

These are
k
conditions, but recall that we also have a
k+1
-st condition

(3.45)

\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{k}}=0.

(
3.44
) and (
3.45
) are linear equations on
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})
. So we have by
Lemma

3.19
and
Lemma

A.3
,

(3.46)

\begin{split}&\dim\{(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}:\quad\text{ \eqref{eq:Sz04:l5:Tangentspace}, \eqref{eq:la5:equation}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationzero} hold}\}\\
\leq&\underbrace{\dim\mathfrak{K}}_{=\aleph}+\dim\{(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\mathfrak{Z}:\quad\text{ \eqref{eq:Sz04:l5:Tangentspacev2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationv2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationzero} hold}\}\end{split}

where

(3.47)

\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{{i_{\ell}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell}}C_{{i_{\ell}}}=0,\quad\ell=1,\ldots,k.

and

(3.48)

\begin{split}Z_{\mathfrak{l}}=&\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\mathfrak{l}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa_{\mathfrak{l}}}C_{\mathfrak{l}}\end{split}

Next we observe that the map

\psi(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\coloneqq(\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa_{1}}}Z_{1},\frac{Z_{2}-Z_{1}}{\overline{\kappa_{2}}},\ldots,\frac{Z_{\aleph}-Z_{\aleph-1}}{\overline{\kappa_{\aleph}}})

Then

\psi:\mathfrak{Z}\to\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}\}

is bijective (recall
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{i}}
from
Lemma

3.18
). Thus,

\begin{split}&\dim\{(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph})\in\mathfrak{Z}:\quad\text{ \eqref{eq:Sz04:l5:Tangentspacev2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationv2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationzero} hold}\}\\
=&\dim\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:la5:equationv2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationzero} hold}\}.\end{split}

In view of this and (
3.46
), our goal (
3.42
) becomes

(3.49)

\dim\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:la5:equationv2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationzero} hold}\}\leq{\aleph}\,(Mn+n-1)-2Mn-Mnk

Observe

{\aleph}\,(Mn+n-1)-2Mn-Mnk\geq 0\Leftarrow\aleph\geq 2M\frac{n}{n-1},

which is always ensured since by assumption
\aleph\geq 2M
.

We can diagonalize (
3.47
) and (
3.45
). Subtract the
(\ell-1)
st line from the
\ell
-th line we find

(3.50)

0=\begin{cases}\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{{i_{1}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa}_{i_{1}}C_{{i_{1}}}\\
(1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell-1}})C_{i_{\ell-1}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=i_{\ell-1}+1}^{{i_{\ell}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}+\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell}}C_{{i_{\ell}}}\quad&\ell=2,\ldots,k\\
(1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{k}})C_{i_{k}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=i_{k}+1}^{\aleph}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\end{cases}

The
C_{i_{\ell}}
and
C_{\aleph}
are uniquely determined in the
\ell
-th equation.
So we can rewrite this further as (modification if
i_{k}=\aleph
)

(3.51)

\begin{cases}C_{{i_{1}}}=-\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{i_{1}}}\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{{i_{1}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\\
C_{{i_{\ell}}}+\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell}}}\left((1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell-1}})C_{i_{\ell-1}}\right)=-\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{i_{\ell}}}\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=i_{\ell-1}+1}^{{i_{\ell}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\quad&\ell=2,\ldots,k\\
C_{\aleph}+\left((1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{k}})C_{i_{k}}\right)=-\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=i_{k}+1}^{\aleph-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\right)\end{cases}

Let
1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{\aleph-k-1}\leq\aleph
be the coefficients complementary to
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},\aleph)
. We can write (
3.51
) as

(3.52)

A\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C_{i_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
C_{i_{k}}\\
C_{\aleph}\end{array}\right)=B\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C_{j_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
C_{i_{k}}\\
C_{j_{\aleph-k-1}}\end{array}\right),

where the coefficient matrix on the left-hand side acting is

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}1&0&0&\ldots&0&0&0\\
\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{i_{2}}}(1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{1}})&1&0&\ldots&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{i_{3}}}(1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{2}})&1&\ldots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\ldots&\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{i_{k}}}(1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{k-1}})&1&0\\
0&0&0&\ldots&0&(1-\overline{\kappa}_{i_{k}})&1\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(k+1)}

and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+1)\times(\aleph-k-1)}
. Then (
3.52
) is equivalent to

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C_{i_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
C_{i_{k}}\\
C_{\aleph}\end{array}\right)=A^{-1}B\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C_{j_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
C_{i_{k}}\\
C_{j_{\aleph-k-1}}\end{array}\right)

and consequently,

\begin{split}&\dim\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:la5:equationv2zero}, \eqref{eq:la5:equationzero} hold}\}\\
=&\dim\{(C_{j_{1}},\ldots,C_{j_{\aleph-k-1}}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:sz5:linesvaskdj} holds}\},\\
\end{split}

where

(3.53)

\begin{cases}0=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{{i_{1}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\\
0=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=i_{\ell-1}+1}^{{i_{\ell}}-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\quad&\ell=2,\ldots,k\\
0=\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=i_{k}+1}^{\aleph-1}C_{\mathfrak{k}}\end{cases}

Each equation is now completely independent of the other, and since the dimension of
\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}}
is
(n(M+1)-1)
,
Lemma

3.9
, a rough estimate is

\dim\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:sz5:linesvaskdj} holds}\}\leq(\aleph-(k+1))\left(n(M+1)-1\right)

Compare this with our desired equation (
3.49
),

\begin{split}&(\aleph-(k+1))\left(n(M+1)-1\right)\leq{\aleph}\,(Mn+n-1)-2Mn-Mnk\\
\Leftrightarrow&k\geq M\frac{n}{n-1}-1.\end{split}

That is our rough estimate establishes
(
3.49
), but only for any
k\geq M\frac{n}{n-1}-1
.

To get the result for all
k
we need to be more careful.

Define
A_{\ell}
to be the number of independent terms in the
\ell
-th line of (
3.53
), i.e.

A_{\ell}=\begin{cases}i_{1}-1\quad&\ell=1\\
i_{\ell}-i_{\ell-1}-1\quad&\ell=2,\ldots,k\\
\aleph-i_{k}-1\end{cases}

observe that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{k+1}A_{\ell}=\aleph-(k+1)
.
Set
B_{\ell}
the number of many consecutive pairs
C_{\mathfrak{i}},C_{\mathfrak{i}+1}
in the
\ell
-line of (
3.53
), i.e., set

B_{\ell}=\left\lfloor\frac{A_{\ell}}{2}\right\rfloor.

From
Lemma

3.11
we have for some
\lambda\in(1,2)
the space

\{(C_{\mathfrak{i}},C_{\mathfrak{i}+1})\in\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}}\times\mathfrak{C}^{\mathfrak{i}+1}\colon C_{\mathfrak{i}}+C_{\mathfrak{i}+1}=Y\}

has dimension at most
\lambda(n(M+1)-1)
. Observe that we can assume
\lambda\approx 2
.

Then the
\ell
-th line has a solution space of dimension

\begin{split}&\dim\{(C_{i_{\ell-1}+1},\ldots,C_{i_{\ell}-1})\in\mathfrak{C}_{i_{\ell-1}+1}\times\ldots\times\mathfrak{C}_{i_{\ell}-1}:\quad\text{$\ell$-th line of \eqref{eq:sz5:linesvaskdj} holds}\}\\
\leq&B_{\ell}\lambda(n(M+1)-1)+(A_{\ell}-2B_{\ell})(n(M+1)-1).\end{split}

Observe that
(A_{\ell}-2B_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}
. Let
L\leq k+1
be the number which counts how many times
A_{\ell}-2B_{\ell}\neq 0
,

L=\#\{\ell\in\{1,\ldots,k+1\}:\quad A_{\ell}-2B_{\ell}=1\}\leq k+1.

Clearly

L+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{k+1}B_{\ell}=\aleph-k-1.

We also observe that
\aleph-(k+1)-L
must necessarily be even. In total we get a better estimate for the dimension of the solution space (better since
\lambda<2
)

\begin{split}&\dim\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:sz5:linesvaskdj} holds}\}\\
\leq&\sum_{\ell=1}^{k+1}B_{\ell}\lambda(n(M+1)-1)+(A_{\ell}-2B_{\ell})(n(M+1)-1)\\
=&(\aleph-k-1-L)\frac{\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)+L(n(M+1)-1)\\
=&(\aleph-k-1)\frac{\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)+L(\frac{2-\lambda}{2})(n(M+1)-1)\\
\end{split}

Since
\lambda<2
the worst case is when
L
is maximal, where the restrictions on
L
are

\begin{cases}L\leq k+1\\
L+k+1\leq\aleph\\
\aleph-(k+1)-L\quad\text{is even}.\end{cases}

So we have the estimate

\begin{split}&\dim\{(C_{1},\ldots,C_{\aleph}):C_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathfrak{i}},\quad\text{\eqref{eq:sz5:linesvaskdj} holds}\}\\
\leq&(\aleph-k-1)\frac{\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)+(k+1)(\frac{2-\lambda}{2})(n(M+1)-1)\\
\end{split}

We compare this to condition (
3.49
),

\begin{split}&(\aleph-k-1)\frac{\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)+(k+1)(\frac{2-\lambda}{2})(n(M+1)-1)\leq\aleph(Mn+n-1)-2Mn-Mnk\\
\Leftrightarrow&(k+1)\left((\frac{2-2\lambda}{2})(n(M+1)-1)+Mn\right)+Mn\leq\aleph\frac{2-\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)\\
\Leftrightarrow&(k+1)\left((2-\lambda)nM-(\lambda-1)(n-1)\right)+Mn\leq\aleph\frac{2-\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)\\
\end{split}

We can ensure that the first term on the left-hand side is negative. Namely we may assume w.l.o.g.
\lambda<2
sufficiently close to
2
, namely

(2-\lambda)nM-(\lambda-1)(n-1)\leq 0\Leftrightarrow\frac{2nM+n-1}{Mn+n-1}\leq\lambda,

Then we have

\begin{split}&(\aleph-k-1)\frac{\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)+(k+1)(\frac{2-\lambda}{2})(n(M+1)-1)\leq\aleph(Mn+n-1)-2Mn-Mnk\\
\Leftarrow&Mn\leq\aleph\frac{2-\lambda}{2}(n(M+1)-1)\\
\end{split}

Thus for
\aleph
suitably large (depending on
\lambda<2
,
n
,
M
) the last inequality is true.
That is, (
3.49
) is established in all remaining cases and we can conclude.

∎

4.
The condition (C) and its genericity

Assume that
\aleph\geq\aleph_{0}
and let
\left(\overline{P},(\overline{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\overline{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
be a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration with endpoints
\overline{Z}=(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}
.

Take from from
Proposition

3.16

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
the manifold of
T_{\aleph}
-endpoints around
\overline{Z}
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)
.
For some given
F:\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
take
\mathcal{K}_{F}
from (
3.28
). Here is our version of condition (C) from
[
12
]
.

Definition 4.1

(Condition (C))
.

We say that that a given
T_{\aleph}
- configuration satisfies the condition (C) if for some small
\delta>0

•

\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
and
\mathcal{K}_{F}
meet transversally at
\overline{Z}
, that is

(4.1)

T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}+T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

•

If for any
\mathfrak{k}
we restrict the map
\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}
to the intersection of
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
and
\mathcal{K}_{F}
in a neighborhood of
\overline{Z}
, i.e. if for some small enough
\delta>0
we consider

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}\Big|_{\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap B_{\delta}(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})},

then

(4.2)

{\rm rank\,}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}\Big|_{\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap B_{\delta}(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})}(Z)=2Mn

for all
Z\in\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap B_{\delta}(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})
, i.e. it is a local submersion.

Remark 4.2
.

By
Proposition

3.16
the dimension of
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
is
\aleph n(M+1)
. By
Lemma

3.15
the dimension of
\mathcal{K}_{F}=(K_{F})^{\aleph}
is
Mn\aleph
. If these two manifolds meet transversely, the dimension of the intersection

\dim\left(\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap B_{\delta}(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})\right)=\aleph n(M+1)+Mn\aleph-2Mn\aleph=\aleph n

The rank of
D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}\Big|_{\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap B_{\delta}(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})}
can thus not be larger than
\aleph n
, so a necessary condition to even dream of having condition
(C)
is

\aleph n\geq 2Mn,

that is

\aleph\geq 2M.

By the assumption of non-degeneracy of the
T_{\aleph}
we also need
\aleph\geq n
.

So it makes only sense to work with
T_{\aleph}
-configurations where

\aleph\geq\max\{2M,n\}.

Observe that in the case
M=n=2
from
[
12
,
14
]
this is exactly the assumption that we look for (at least)
T_{4}
-configurations – and as
[
14
]
showed, it is easier (and in some sense: only possible) to find a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration for
\aleph=5
. Our argument below will work with
\aleph
even larger (although for specific
n
,
M
, it might be simple to compute an “optimal”
\aleph
).

A crucial result is that condition
(C)
is generic. Recall that we can always identify
F:\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
F:\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Fix
M,n\geq 2
. There exists
\aleph_{0}=\aleph_{0}(M,n)
such that for any
\aleph\geq\aleph_{0}
the following holds.

Assume
F_{0}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{M\times n},\mathbb{R})
satisfies the strong Legendre-Hadamard condition, cf.
Definition

B.1
, and that

\mathcal{K}_{F_{0}}
contains a non-degenerate
T_{\aleph}
configuration
(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})
with its manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
.

Then for any
\delta>0
there exists
F\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{M\times n},\mathbb{R})
with

(4.3)

\|F-F_{0}\|_{C^{2}}<\delta

such that
F
still satisfies the strong Legendre-Hadamard condition,
(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})
still belongs to
\mathcal{K}_{F}
, but the condition
(C)
is satisfied for
F
.

If
F_{0}
is
strongly
polyconvex then we can ensure that
F
is
strongly
polyconvex.

First we record the following basic observations

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
H_{\mathfrak{k}}\in C_{c}^{2}(B(0,R),\mathbb{R})
and fix some points
(X_{\mathfrak{k}})_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
.
Set

F(X)\coloneqq F_{0}(X)+\delta_{1}\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}H_{\mathfrak{k}}(X-X_{\mathfrak{k}}),

for some
\delta_{1}>0
.

For any
\delta>0
there exists
\delta_{1}>0
such that

•

(
4.3
) holds,

•

if
F_{0}
satisfies the strong Legendre-Hadamard condition, so does
F
,

•

if
F_{0}
is strongly polyconvex, so is
F
,

•

Recall the definition of
K_{F}
from (
2.6
). There exist
R_{0}>0
depending on
(X_{\mathfrak{k}})_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}
such that if
R<R_{0}
for any
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
we have the following: If
DH_{\mathfrak{i}}(0)=0
and if
(X_{\mathfrak{i}},Y_{\mathfrak{i}})\in K_{F_{0}}
for some
Y_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}
then
(X_{\mathfrak{i}},Y_{\mathfrak{i}})\in K_{F_{0}}
.

For the last bullet point, let us stress however that in general
K_{F_{0}}\neq K_{F}

Proof.

(
4.3
) is clearly satisfied if we choose
\delta
suitably small (depending on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathfrak{k}}
).

Since
F_{0}
satisfies the the strong Legendre-Hadamard condition (
B.1
), taking
\delta>0
suitably small (depending on
H_{\mathfrak{k}}
) we can ensure that

\begin{split}\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}\partial_{X_{j\beta}}F(X)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,\eta_{\alpha}\eta_{\beta}=&\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}\partial_{X_{j\beta}}F_{0}(X)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,\eta_{\alpha}\eta_{\beta}+\delta\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}\partial_{X_{i\alpha}}\partial_{X_{j\beta}}H_{\mathfrak{k}}(X-X_{\mathfrak{k}})\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,\eta_{\alpha}\eta_{\beta}\\
\geq&\left(\lambda-\delta\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}\|D^{2}H_{\mathfrak{k}}\|\right)|\xi|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}|\eta|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{2}\\
\geq&\frac{\lambda}{2}|\xi|_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}^{2}|\eta|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{2}.\end{split}

That is
F
satisfies the strong Legendre-Hadamard condition.

If
F_{0}
is polyconvex, i.e. for some
\gamma>0
we know that
F_{0}(X)-\gamma|X|^{2}
is polyconvex, then we observe that

F(X)-\frac{\gamma}{2}|X|^{2}=\underbrace{F_{0}(X)-\gamma|X|^{2}}_{\text{polyconvex}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}|X|^{2}+\delta\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}H_{\mathfrak{k}}(X-X_{\mathfrak{k}}),

which is polyconvex, since for suitably small
\delta

X\mapsto\frac{\gamma}{2}|X|^{2}+\delta\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}H_{\mathfrak{k}}(X-X_{\mathfrak{k}})

is convex.

If
DH_{\mathfrak{i}}(0)=0
, then

DF(X_{\mathfrak{i}})=DF_{0}(X_{\mathfrak{i}})+\delta_{1}\sum_{\mathfrak{k}\neq\mathfrak{i}}DH_{\mathfrak{k}}(X_{\mathfrak{i}}-X_{\mathfrak{k}}),

If
R<\inf_{\mathfrak{l}\neq\mathfrak{k}}|X_{\mathfrak{l}}-X_{\mathfrak{k}}|
then by the support of of
H

DF(X_{\mathfrak{i}})=DF_{0}(X_{\mathfrak{i}}).

Thus the claim follows from the definition (
2.6
).
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
be strongly polyconvex,
\overline{Z}=(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})
be a nondegenerate
T_{\aleph}
-configuration with associated manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
and map map
\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)
from
Proposition

3.16
. Assume moreover that
\overline{Z}\in\mathcal{K}_{F}
.

If for some
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
we have

(4.4)

T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}+\ker D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

then necessarily

\aleph\geq 2M.

More importantly, if (
4.4
) holds for all
\mathfrak{k}
then condition (C) holds at
\overline{Z}
.

Proof.

We have by the rank nullity theorem

\dim\left(\ker D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}\right)=\dim\left(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}\right)-\dim\left({\rm im}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}\right)

From
Proposition

3.16
we have

\dim{\rm im}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}=2Mn

and

\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M})={\aleph}n(M+1),

and thus (recall that
\aleph\geq n\geq 2
)

\dim\left(\ker D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}\right)={\aleph}n(M+1)-2Mn\geq 0

On the other hand by
Lemma

3.15

\dim T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}={\aleph}Mn

Also

\dim\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}=2Mn{\aleph}.

We recall that if
X
and
Y
are linear subspaces then
\dim(X\cap Y)=\dim X+\dim Y-{\rm span}\{X,Y\}
. Thus, (
4.4
) implies

(4.5)

T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap\ker D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}={\aleph}Mn+{\aleph}n(M+1)-2Mn-2Mn\aleph=(\aleph-2M)n

In particular the right-hand side number must be nonnegative, so
\aleph\geq 2M
.

From the rank-nullity equation again we have

\begin{split}&\dim{\rm im}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}}\\
=&\dim\{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\}-\dim\ker D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}}\\
=&\dim T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}+\dim T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}-\dim\left({\rm span}\{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph},T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\}\right)-(\aleph-2M)n\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:laslzlo5:goalv2}}{=}&{\aleph}n(M+1)+{\aleph}Mn-2Mn\aleph-(\aleph-2M)n\\
=&2Mn\\
\end{split}

That is we have

(4.6)

\dim\left({\rm im}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{Z}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{Z}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}}\right)=2Mn\quad\forall\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\},

(
4.6
) readily implies the openness condition of Condition (C), (
4.2
). It remains to establish the transversality condition, (
4.1
)

T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}+T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}.

Observe that by
Lemma

3.15
and
Proposition

3.16

\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F})=\aleph Mn,\quad\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})=\aleph n(M+1)

Since

\dim({\rm span\,}\{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F},T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})=\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F})+\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})-\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})

So we see that we always have

(4.7)

\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})\geq\aleph n,

and transversality is equivalent to

(4.8)

\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})=\aleph n

By the rank-nullity theorem

\dim\left({\rm ker}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}}\right)+\dim\left({\rm im}D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}}\right)=\dim(T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}\cap T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{\aleph})

Then (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) imply (
4.8
), i.e. transversality (
4.1
) is established.
∎

We also have the following, see
[
14
, Lemma 4]
where this is proven for
N=4
. For general
N
the proof is completely analogous.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Suppose
L\subset(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{\aleph}
is a subspace with
\dim L\leq N\aleph

Denote for
1=i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq\aleph
the map

p_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}:(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{\aleph}\to(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{\aleph}

which sets the
i_{j}
-components to be zero, i.e.

p_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\aleph})=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{i_{1}-1},0,v_{i_{1}+1},\ldots,v_{i_{2}-1},0,v_{i_{2}+1},\ldots)

Assume that

(4.9)

\dim\left(L\cap{\rm im}(p_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})\right)\leq N(\aleph-k)

Then for any
(\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}_{sym}
and any
\delta>0
there exists
(A_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}_{sym}
with
|A_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{i}}|<\delta
such that if we set

V_{\mathfrak{i}}\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{c}I_{N\times N}\\
A_{\mathfrak{i}}\end{array}\right)\mathbb{R}^{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2N}

we have

V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\aleph}\cap L=\{0\}.

Proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

For
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
pick

A_{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}

a symmetric linear map to be determined later. We set

F(X)\coloneqq F_{0}(X)+\delta\sum_{\mathfrak{k}=1}^{\aleph}H_{\mathfrak{k}}(X-X_{\mathfrak{k}}),

for suitably small
\delta
,
H_{\mathfrak{k}}
with compact support around
0
and

DH_{\mathfrak{k}}(0)=0,

and

D^{2}H_{\mathfrak{k}}(0)=:A_{\mathfrak{k}}

In view of
Lemma

4.4
we
F
satisfies all conditions besides condition (C).

Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}
the manifold of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations around
\overline{Z}
, cf.
Proposition

3.16
, with associated map
\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathcal{M}_{\aleph}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)
.

In view of
Lemma

3.15
we can write for
\overline{Z}=(\overline{Z}_{1},\ldots,\overline{Z}_{\aleph})

T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{K}_{F}=V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\aleph}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

where

V_{\mathfrak{i}}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{X}\\
A_{\mathfrak{i}}[\tilde{X}]\end{array}\right):\quad\tilde{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\right\}

Our goal will be to choose the symmetric linear map
A_{\mathfrak{i}}:\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\to\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}
,
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
such that for all
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

(4.10)

V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\aleph}+\ker D\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(\overline{Z})\Big|_{T_{\overline{Z}}\mathcal{M}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}

holds, since then, by
Lemma

4.5
, condition (C) is satisfied.

Now assume we have chosen
(A_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}
such that (
4.10
) holds for some
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
. Then for all
(\tilde{A}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}
with
|A_{\mathfrak{i}}-\tilde{A}_{\mathfrak{i}}|\ll 1
condition (
4.10
) still holds for the same
\mathfrak{k}
.

We are now going to show that given any tuple
(\overline{A}_{1},\ldots,\overline{A}_{\aleph})
with corresponding spaces
\overline{V}_{1},\ldots,\overline{V}_{\aleph}
, any
\delta>0
and any
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
we can find
(A_{1},\ldots,A_{\aleph})
and corresponding spaces
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{\aleph})
such that
|A_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{i}}|<\delta
and (
4.10
) holds for
\mathfrak{k}
. If we are able to do this, we can conclude by induction that we can find
V_{1},\ldots,V_{\aleph}
so that (
4.10
) holds.

It suffices to do this for
\mathfrak{k}=1
.
Fix
(\overline{A}_{1},\ldots,\overline{A}_{\aleph})
and
\delta>0

We need to show that there exists
L\subset\ker D\pi_{1}(\overline{Z})\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M\times n}\\
\end{array}\right)^{\aleph}
such that

\dim L\geq\aleph Mn

and there are
(A_{1},\ldots,A_{\aleph})
and corresponding subspaces
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{\aleph})
with
|A_{\mathfrak{i}}-\overline{A}_{\mathfrak{i}}|<\delta
such that

L\cap(V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\aleph})=\{0\}

If we have this then

\dim(L+(V_{1}\times\ldots V_{\aleph})=\dim(L)+\dim(V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\aleph})-\dim(L\cap(V_{1}\times\ldots\times V_{\aleph}))

implies (since
\dim V_{\mathfrak{i}}=Mn
)

\dim(L+(V_{1}\times\ldots V_{\aleph})=2\aleph Mn

and thus (
4.10
) holds for
\kappa=1
.

We now apply
Lemma

3.20
, which implies that the conditions of
Lemma

4.6
are met for
N=Mn
. The latter lemma then implies the existence of
L
as desired. We can conclude.
∎

5.
On
\mathscr{R}
-convex hulls, laminates, and laminates of finite order

Since we replaced the notion of “rank-one” in
Section

3.1
(for the benefit of later being able to use
Lemma

6.1
), we need to replace the notion of rank-one convex functions, and re-establish the important properties that were used in
[
12
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A function
f:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\to\mathbb{R}
is
\mathscr{R}
-convex if

f(tA+(1-t)B)\leq tf(A)+(1-t)f(B)\quad\forall t\in[0,1].

holds whenever
A,B\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
with
A-B\in\mathscr{R}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Any
\mathscr{R}
-convex function is locally Lipschitz.

Proof.

Since
f
is
\mathscr{R}
-convex,

\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto f(A+tC)

is locally Lipschitz for any
A\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
and
C\in\mathscr{R}
.

General local Lipschitz continuity is a consequence of
Lemma

3.4
.
∎

Definition 5.3

(
\mathscr{R}
-convex hull)
.

Let
K\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
be compact.
Then we define its
\mathscr{R}
-convex hull
K^{r}
as follows:

K^{\mathscr{R}c}\coloneqq\left\{X\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right):\quad f(X)\leq\sup_{K}f\quad\forall f:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\to\mathbb{R}\quad\text{rank-one convex}\right\}.

For general sets
U\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
the
\mathscr{R}
-convex hull is defined as

U^{\mathscr{R}c}\coloneqq\bigcup_{K\subset U\text{ compact}}K^{\mathscr{R}c}.

Definition 5.4

(Laminates and laminates of finite order)
.

In analogy to
[
12
]
we define laminates and laminates of finite order, simply replacing rank-one convexity by
\mathscr{R}
-convexity:

•

Denote by
\mathcal{P}
the set of all compactly supported probability measures in
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
.

•

For a compact set
K\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
,
\mathcal{P}(K)
denotes the set of all measures in
\mathcal{P}
that are supported in
K
.

•

For a radon measure
\nu
we denote by
\overline{\nu}
its baricenter

\overline{\nu}=\int Xd\nu(X).

•

A measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}
is a
laminate
if

f(\overline{\nu})\leq\int fd\nu\quad\text{ for all $\mathscr{R}$-convex $f$}

•

For
K\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
we denote by

\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)=\left\{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(K):\text{$\nu$ is laminate}\right\}

•

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
be an open set.
Assume that
\nu\in\mathcal{P}
is of the form

\nu=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}\delta_{A_{j}}

where
A_{j}\in\mathcal{O}
, and
A_{j}\neq A_{k}
for
j\neq k
,
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}=1
.

Another measure
\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
can be obtained from
\nu
by an
elementary splitting in
\mathcal{O}
if for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and some
\lambda\in[0,1]
there exists
B_{2},B_{1}\in\mathcal{O}
,
[B_{1},B_{2}]\in\mathcal{O}
, with
B_{2}-B_{1}\in\mathscr{R}
, and

A_{i}=(1-s)B_{1}+sB_{2}

and

\begin{split}\nu^{\prime}=&\nu+\lambda\lambda_{i}\left((1-s)\delta_{B_{1}}+s\delta_{B_{2}}-\delta_{A_{i}}\right)\\
=&\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{r}\lambda_{j}\delta_{A_{j}}+\lambda_{i}(1-\lambda)\delta_{A_{i}}+\lambda\lambda_{i}(1-s)\delta_{B_{1}}+\lambda\lambda_{i}s\delta_{B_{2}}\end{split}

•

A
laminate of finite order
in
\mathcal{O}
is a measure
\nu
for which there exists a finite sequence of measures
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}=\nu
such that
\nu_{1}=\delta_{A}
, and
\nu_{i}
can be obtained from
\nu_{i-1}
by an elementary splitting in
\mathcal{O}
as above. The set of laminates of finite order in
\mathcal{O}
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
.

A few simple observations:

Lemma 5.5
.

(1)

If
\nu^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\nu
by an elementary splitting, then

\overline{\nu}=\overline{\nu^{\prime}}.

(2)

If
\nu
is a laminate and
\nu^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\nu
by an elementary splitting, then
\nu^{\prime}
is also a laminate.

(3)

Any laminate of finite order is also a laminate.

(4)

Assume that
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
and
[\overline{\nu}_{1},\overline{\nu}_{2}]\subset\mathcal{O}
and
\overline{\nu}_{1}-\overline{\nu}_{2}\in\mathscr{R}
.

Fix
\lambda\in(0,1)
and set

\nu_{3}\coloneqq\lambda\nu_{1}+(1-\lambda)\nu_{2}

Then
\nu_{3}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
.
In particular,
[
12
, Lemma 2.2]
still holds for our notion of
\mathscr{R}
-convex.

(5)

If
B_{1},B_{2}\in K^{\mathscr{R}c}
and
B_{2}-B_{1}\in\mathscr{R}
then

[B_{1},B_{2}]\subset K^{\mathscr{R}c}.

(6)

for any set
U
and any
X\in U
we have
X\in U^{\mathscr{R}c}
.

Proof.

(1)

obvious

(2)

Let
f
be
\mathscr{R}
-convex, and assume

f(\overline{\nu})\leq\int fd\nu=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}f(A_{j})

Assume the splitting happens at
A_{i}=(1-s)B_{1}+sB_{2}
with
B_{2}-B_{1}\in\mathscr{R}
then we have by
\mathscr{R}
-convexity

\lambda_{i}\lambda f(A_{i})\leq(1-s)\lambda_{i}\lambda f(B_{1})+s\lambda_{i}\lambda f(B_{2}),

so that (recall:
\overline{\nu^{\prime}}=\overline{\nu}
) we have

\begin{split}f(\overline{\nu^{\prime}})\leq&\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{r}\lambda_{j}f(A_{j})+\lambda_{i}(1-\lambda)f(A_{i})+(1-s)\lambda_{i}\lambda f(B_{1})+s\lambda_{i}\lambda f(B_{2})\\
=&\int fd\nu^{\prime}.\end{split}

(3)

obvious since
\delta_{A}
is a trivially laminate.

(4)

Set

P_{1}\coloneqq\overline{\nu_{1}},\quad P_{2}\coloneqq\overline{\nu_{2}},

and

P_{3}\coloneqq\overline{\nu_{3}}=\lambda P_{1}+(1-\lambda)P_{2}.

Set

\mu_{0}=\delta_{\overline{\nu_{3}}}

Then we set

\mu_{1}=\lambda\delta_{P_{1}}+(1-\lambda)\delta_{P_{2}}.

Clearly
\mu_{1}
can be obtained by an elementary splitting from
\mu_{0}
. Now we simply do the construction steps for
\delta_{P_{1}}
as in
\nu_{1}
and
\delta_{P_{2}}
as in
\nu_{2}
.

(5)

Assume that
B_{1},B_{2}\in K^{\mathscr{R}c}
and
B_{1}-B_{2}\in\mathscr{R}
.

Take any
B=sB_{1}+(1-s)B_{2}
for some
s\in[0,1]
. We need to show that
B\in K^{\mathscr{R}c}
.

Let
f:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\to\mathbb{R}
be
\mathscr{R}
-convex. Since
B_{1}-B_{2}\in\mathscr{R}

f(B)\leq sf(B_{1})+(1-s)f(B_{2})

Since
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are in
K^{\mathscr{R}c}

f(B)\leq s\sup_{K}f+(1-s)\sup_{K}f=\sup_{K}f

This holds for any
\mathscr{R}
-convex
f
, so
B\in K^{\mathscr{R}c}
.

(6)

obvious

∎

We can characterize the
\mathscr{R}
-convex hull of a compact set
K
by baricenters of measures in
\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
K\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
be compact. Then

K^{\mathscr{R}c}=\left\{\overline{\nu}:\quad\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)\right\}

Proof.

\supseteq
:
Assume
\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)
, then
f(\overline{\nu})\leq\int fd\nu
for all
\mathscr{R}
-convex
f
. In particular

f(\overline{\nu})\leq\sup_{K}f

and thus
\overline{\nu}\in K^{r}
.

\subseteq
Let
P\in K^{r}
. We need to find
\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)
, with
\overline{\nu}=P
.

Let

d_{K}(X)\coloneqq{\rm dist\,}(X,K),\quad X\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right).

Let
\mathcal{O}\supset K
be an open, bounded set that contains
P
.

We define the
\mathscr{R}
-convex envelope for some open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
as

(5.1)

R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(X)\coloneqq\sup\{g(X):\quad g:\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}\quad\text{$\mathscr{R}$-convex in $\mathcal{O}$ and $g\leq d_{K}$ in $\mathcal{O}$}\}.

R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(X)
is
\mathscr{R}
-convex,
and by
[
12
, Lemma 2.3]
(whose proof can be copied verbatim in our case) we can assume
R_{\mathcal{O}}d
is defined in all of
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
– and globally
\mathscr{R}
-convex.

Also since
g(X)\equiv 0\leq d_{K}(X)
is
\mathscr{R}
-convex, we have

R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(X)\geq 0\quad\text{in $\mathscr{O}$}.

Since
P\in K^{r}
by assumption, we find

R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(P)\leq 0.

Consequently,

R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(X)=0.

On the other hand, by
[
12
, Lemma 2.2]
(which holds since we have
Lemma

5.5
)

(5.2)

R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(X)=\inf\left\{\int d_{K}\ d\nu:\quad\nu\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O}):\quad\overline{\nu}=X\right\}.

where
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
denotes laminates of finite order,
Definition

5.4
.

Thus, from (
5.2
) for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
we find some
\nu_{k}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
such that
\overline{\nu}_{k}=P
and

0=R_{\mathcal{O}}d_{K}(P)\geq\int d_{K}\,d\nu_{k}-\frac{1}{k}.

Since
\nu_{k}
are Radon measures, up to not relabelled subsequence, they converge weak* to some Radon measure
\nu
and we have
\overline{\nu}=p
, and

0=\int d_{K}\,d\nu.

Since
d_{K}
is zero exactly in
K
, we conclude that
\nu
must be supported in
K
, that is
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(K)
. The last property we need to show is that
\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)
, i.e. that
\nu
is a laminate in the sense of
Definition

5.4
:

And indeed, each
\nu_{k}
is a laminate, so we have for any
\mathscr{R}
-convex
f
(which is necessarily continuous by
Lemma

5.2
)

f(P)=f(\overline{\nu_{k}})\leq\int f\,d\nu_{k}.

The right-hand side converges and we have

f(P)\leq\int f\,d\nu.

This holds for any
\mathscr{R}
-convex
f
, so
\nu
is a laminate in the definition of
Definition

5.4
, and thus we have found
\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(K)
, with
\overline{\nu}=P
. We can conclude.
∎

Recall the notion of
T_{\aleph}
-configurations in
Definition

3.12
. We begin with the following observation which takes the role of
[
12
, Theorem 2.1.]
for us.

Lemma 5.7
.

Assume that
\left(\tilde{P},(\tilde{C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
is a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration in the sense of
Definition

3.12
.

Denote the endpoints by

Z_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})

and the base points by

P_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})\coloneqq P+C_{1}+C_{2}+\ldots C_{\mathfrak{k}-1}

Fix
\mathfrak{k}
, then there exist
(\lambda_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\subset(0,1)
,
\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{i}}=1
, such that

\nu_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{i}}\delta_{Z_{\mathfrak{i}}}

is a laminate and

\overline{\nu_{\mathfrak{k}}}=P_{\mathfrak{k}}.

\nu
is in general
not
a laminate of finite order, however for any open set
\mathcal{O}
containing
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}
there exists a sequence of laminates of finite order
\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
such that

\nu_{i}\text{ weak* converges to }\nu

and

\overline{\nu_{i}}=P_{\mathfrak{k}},

and they can be written as

\nu_{i}=\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{l}}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{Z_{\mathfrak{l}}}

where

•

\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i},\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}\in[0,1]

•

i\mapsto\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}
are non-decreasing for each
\mathfrak{l}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

•

i\mapsto\max_{\mathfrak{l}}\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}
is non-increasing and converges to zero as
i\to\infty
.

•

for each
i
, the coefficient
\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}\neq 0
for at most one
\mathfrak{l}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}

•

we have

\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}+\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}=1.

•

We have

\lim_{i\to\infty}\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}=1

Proof.

We observe that for each
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
there is
s_{\mathfrak{i}}\in(0,1)
(since
\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}>1
)

P_{\mathfrak{i}}=s_{\mathfrak{i}}P_{\mathfrak{i}-1}+(1-s_{\mathfrak{i}})Z_{\mathfrak{i}-1}

And

P_{\mathfrak{i}-1}-Z_{\mathfrak{i}-1}\in\mathscr{R}.

We start by setting

\mu_{0}\coloneqq\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{k}}}.

\mu_{0}
is clearly a laminate of finite order. And by the above argument we can do an elementary splitting to get

\mu_{1}=s_{\mathfrak{k}}\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{k}-1}}+(1-s_{\mathfrak{k}})\delta_{Z_{\mathfrak{k}-1}}

Let us stress that
s_{\mathfrak{k}}\neq 0,1
.

We now iteratively perform an elementary splitting the
P_{\mathfrak{i}}
-term (there is exactly one in each step), and find a sequence
\mu_{k}
of laminates of finite order of the form

\mu_{k}=t_{k}\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{i}_{k}}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{i};k}\delta_{Z_{\mathfrak{i}}}

where
t_{k}+\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{i};k}=1
,
t_{k}\in(0,1)
and
\lambda_{\mathfrak{i};k}\in[0,1]
– but
k\mapsto\lambda_{\mathfrak{i};k}
is non-decreasing. After
\aleph
iterations of this arguments all
\lambda_{\mathfrak{i};k}>0
.
We also have

t_{k+1}\leq t_{k}\max_{\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}}s_{\mathfrak{i}}

and thus
t_{k}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0
, and
\lambda_{\mathfrak{i};k}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}\lambda_{\mathfrak{i}}\in(0,1)
.

Since
\mu_{k}
is a Radon measure (up to subsequence) it weak* converges to some
\mu
.

Since each
\mu_{k}
is a laminate of finite order, each
\mu_{k}
is in particular a laminate, and thus for any (necessarily continuous)
\mathscr{R}
-convex
f

f(\overline{P_{\mathfrak{k}}})=f(\overline{\mu_{k}})\leq\int fd\mu_{k}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}\int fd\mu.

That is,

\mu=\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{i}}\delta_{Z_{\mathfrak{i}}}\in\mathcal{P}^{\ell}(\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph}\})

is a laminate (not necessarily of finite order) with
\overline{\mu}=P_{\mathfrak{k}}
.

In particular, in view of
Proposition

5.6

P_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}.

This holds for any
\mathfrak{k}
so we actually have

\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{\aleph}\}\subset\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}.

Take now any open set
\mathcal{O}
containing
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}
. In view of
Lemma

5.5
any of the elementary splittings above are within
\mathcal{O}
. Thus,
\mu_{k}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
.

We can conclude.

∎

We also have

Lemma 5.8
.

Assume that
\left({P},({C}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},({\kappa}_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph}\right)
is a
T_{\aleph}
-configuration in the sense of
Definition

3.12
.

Denote the endpoints by

Z_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})

and the base points by

P_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})

Let
\mu\in(0,1)
such that
\mu\kappa_{\mathfrak{i}}>1

Then

•

we have

\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq(1-\mu)P_{\mathfrak{k}}+\mu Z_{\mathfrak{k}}=\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{i})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\lambda\kappa_{i})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})

•

If we set for
\lambda\in[0,\mu]
.

\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq(1-\lambda)P_{\mathfrak{k}}+\lambda Z_{\mathfrak{k}}

then

\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\{\tilde{Z}_{1},\tilde{Z}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}

for any
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
.

•

For any
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
, any
\mathcal{O}
open such that
\mathcal{O}\supset\{\tilde{Z}_{1},\tilde{Z}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}
there exists a laminate of finite order
\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
with
\overline{\sigma_{i}}=\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
\sigma_{i}
weak* converges to a laminate

(1-\frac{\lambda}{\mu})\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{l}}}+\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}},

where
\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}}\in(0,1)
and
\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l}}=1
.

\sigma_{i}
has the form

\sigma_{i}\coloneqq(1-\frac{\lambda}{\mu})\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{l}}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{l}}}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}}

Proof.

We have

P_{\mathfrak{k}}=P+\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\mathfrak{k}-1}C_{\mathfrak{i}}

and

\begin{split}Z_{\mathfrak{k}}=&P+\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\mathfrak{k}-1}C_{\mathfrak{i}}+\kappa C_{\mathfrak{k}}.\end{split}

So,

\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\coloneqq(1-\mu)P_{\mathfrak{k}}+\mu Z_{\mathfrak{k}}=P+\sum_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\mathfrak{k}-1}C_{\mathfrak{i}}+\mu\kappa_{\mathfrak{k}}C_{\mathfrak{k}}=\phi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{i})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\lambda\kappa_{i})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})

In particular we have that
(\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph})
are endpoints of a
\aleph
-configuration, and we notice that

\pi_{\mathfrak{k}}(P,(C_{i})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph},(\lambda\kappa_{i})_{\mathfrak{i}=1}^{\aleph})=P_{\mathfrak{k}}

By
Lemma

5.7
we have
P_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\{\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}
,
Lemma

5.5
we have
\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\{\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}
and – since
\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}-P_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathscr{R}

[P_{\mathfrak{k}},\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}]\in\{\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph}\}^{\mathscr{R}c}.

We observe that
\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\in[P_{\mathfrak{k}},\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}]
, because
\lambda\leq\mu
and

\begin{split}\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}=&(1-\lambda)P_{\mathfrak{k}}+\lambda Z_{\mathfrak{k}}\\
=&(1-\frac{\lambda}{\mu})P_{\mathfrak{k}}+\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}.\end{split}

For a fixed
\mathfrak{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\aleph\}
we take from
Lemma

5.7
the laminate of finite order

\nu_{i}=\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{l}}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{l}}}

such that

\overline{\nu_{i}}=P_{\mathfrak{k}}

Then

\sigma_{i}\coloneqq(1-\frac{\lambda}{\mu})\left(\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\mu_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{l}}}+\sum_{\mathfrak{l}=1}^{\aleph}\lambda_{\mathfrak{l};i}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{l}}}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}}

satisfies

\overline{\sigma_{i}}=\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Moreover,
\sigma_{i}
is a laminate of finite order: We can set

\sigma_{i;0}\coloneqq\delta_{\overline{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}}

and

\sigma_{i;1}\coloneqq(1-\frac{\lambda}{\mu})\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{k}}}+\frac{\lambda}{\mu}\delta_{\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}}

which we can obtain by elementary splitting since
P_{\mathfrak{k}}-\tilde{Z}_{\mathfrak{k}}\in\mathscr{R}
. Now we continue to define
\sigma_{i;1}
by going from
\delta_{P_{\mathfrak{k}}}
to
\nu_{i}
via elementary splittings (which is possible since
\nu_{i}
is a laminate of finite order).

Thus
\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{O})
for any open set
\mathcal{O}\supset\{\tilde{Z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{Z}_{\aleph}\}
.

∎

6.
Basic construction: the wiggle lemmata

We are going to consider maps
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
.
As usual we call such a map
affine linear
if it is differentiable everywhere and

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)\ni\partial_{\alpha}F\text{ is constant}

We will often call a given affine linear maps

{\rm Aff}(x).

A map
f:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)
is called
piecewise affine linear
if it is locally Lipschitz and there are countably many
(\Omega_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}
of open and pairwise disjoint subsets of
\Omega
such that

\mathcal{L}^{n}(\Omega\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{j})=0

and
f\Big|_{\Omega_{j}}
is affine linear.

The main issue, that is responsible for the linear algebra torture we have to deal with, is that if
n>2
then
df
and
\nabla f
are two different operations – which is the reason we chose
\mathscr{R}
in
Definition

3.1
in the way we did.

Namely, we have the following version of the “wiggle” lemma (
[
12
, Lemma 3.1]
,
[
1
, Lemma 2.1]
).

Lemma 6.1
.

Let

A_{1},A_{2}\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-1}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that
A_{1}-A_{2}\in\mathscr{R}
. For
\lambda\in(0,1)
set
C\coloneqq(1-\lambda)A_{1}+\lambda A_{2}
.

Also assume that we have any affine linear map on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

{\rm Aff}(x):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that

d({\rm Aff}(x))\equiv C.

Then for any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
there exists a piecewise affine mapping

f:\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that

•

(6.1)

{\rm dist\,}(df,\{A_{1},A_{2}\})<\delta\quad\text{a.e. in $\Omega$}

•

(6.2)

\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\precsim_{|A_{1}|,|A_{2}|,|\nabla{\rm Aff}|}1.

•

If
A_{1}-A_{2}\neq 0
then we have

(6.3)

|\{x\in\Omega:|df(x)-A_{1}|<\delta\}|=(1-\lambda)|\Omega|

and

(6.4)

|\{x\in\Omega:|df(x)-A_{2}|<\delta\}|=\lambda|\Omega|

•

(6.5)

\left|{\rm Aff}(x)-f\right|<\varepsilon\quad\text{in $\Omega$}

and

f={\rm Aff}(x)\quad\text{on $\partial\Omega$}

Proof.

If
A_{1}=A_{2}
(and thus
A_{1}=A_{2}=C
) then we set
f\coloneqq{\rm Aff}(x)
which satisfies all the assumptions. So assume from now on
A_{1}\neq A_{2}
.

For
\lambda\in(0,1)
let
s:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the Lipschitz map defined as

s(t)\coloneqq\left(\lambda(1-\lambda)+t[(1-\lambda)\chi_{(-\lambda,0)}(t)-\lambda\chi_{(0,1-\lambda)}(t)\right)\chi_{(-\lambda,1-\lambda)}(t).

Then

s^{\prime}(t)=\begin{cases}1-\lambda\quad&t\in(-\lambda,0)\\
-\lambda\quad&t\in(0,1-\lambda)\\
0\quad&\text{a.e. otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since
A_{2}-A_{1}\in\mathscr{R}
, cf. (
3.1
), we find some
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
a\in\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{M}\\
\mathbb{R}^{M}\otimes\bigwedge\nolimits^{n-2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{array}\right)
such that

A_{2}-A_{1}=b_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a.

If
b=0
then
A_{1}=A_{2}
, so by assumption
b\neq 0
. Simply by otherwise redefining
a
as
|b|a
, we may assume w.l.o.g. that
|b|=1
. Pick
o^{2},\ldots,o^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
(b,o^{2},\ldots,o^{n})
is an orthonormal basis.

For
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
\sigma\in(0,1]
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we set

w_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)\coloneqq\sigma\gamma\left(s\left(\frac{q\cdot(x-p)}{\sigma\gamma}\right)-\sum_{i=2}^{n}|o^{i}\cdot(x-p)/\gamma|\right)

We observe that
w
is piecewise affine linear.

Moreover,

dw_{\gamma,\sigma,p}=s^{\prime}\left(\frac{q\cdot(x-p)}{\sigma\gamma}\right)q_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}+O_{\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}}(\sigma).

and

(6.6)

\partial_{\beta}w_{\gamma,\sigma,p}=s^{\prime}\left(\frac{q\cdot(x-p)}{\sigma\gamma}\right)q_{\beta}+O(\sigma).

So if we set

f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)={\rm Aff}(x)+w_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)a

then we have

\begin{split}df_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)=&C+s^{\prime}\left(\frac{q\cdot(x-p)}{\sigma\gamma}\right)\,q_{\alpha}\,dx^{\alpha}\wedge a+O(\sigma)\\
=&C+s^{\prime}\left(\frac{q\cdot(x-p)}{\sigma\gamma}\right)\left(A_{2}-A_{1}\right)+O(\sigma)\\
=&\begin{cases}A_{2}+O(\sigma)\quad&\text{if }q\cdot(x-p)\in(-\sigma\gamma\lambda,0)\\
A_{1}+O(\sigma)\quad&\text{if }q\cdot(x-p)\in(0,(1-\lambda)\sigma\gamma)\\
C+O(\sigma)\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{split}

We also need to control
\nabla f
, but a rough estimate suffices, so with (
6.6
) we obtain
for any
\gamma,\sigma\in(0,1]
(recall that
|q|=1
)

|\nabla\left(f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)-{\rm Aff}(x)\right)|\leq_{a}1.

The set

\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:w_{\gamma,\sigma,p}>0\}=b+\sigma\Omega_{\gamma,1,0}

is certainly an open set (it is even a polytope, i.e. the convex hull of finitely many points). It is also bounded.
Trivially we have

(6.7)

f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)={\rm Aff}(x)\quad\text{on $\partial\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}$}

In conclusion, we have
f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}
is Lipschitz in
\overline{\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}}
, and

\begin{split}df_{\gamma,\sigma,p}=&\begin{cases}A_{2}+O(\sigma)\quad&\text{if }q\cdot(x-p)\in(-\sigma\gamma\lambda,0)\\
A_{1}+O(\sigma)\quad&\text{if }q\cdot(x-p)\in(0,(1-\lambda)\sigma\gamma)\\
C+O(\sigma)\quad&\text{a.e. otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Since the last case is a zero-set in
\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}
and
\partial\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}
is a zero-set we find that

(6.8)

{\rm dist\,}(df_{\gamma,\sigma,p},\{A_{1},A_{2}\})\leq O(\sigma)\quad\text{a.e. in $\overline{\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}}$}

We also observe that (for
\sigma
so small that
{\rm dist\,}(A,B)\gg O(\sigma)
) – since by the definition of
s
we have all
x
with
q\cdot(x-p)\in[-\sigma\gamma\lambda,0]
or
q\cdot(x-p)\in[0,(1-\lambda)\sigma\gamma]
belong to
\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}
,

(6.9)

\left|\{x\in\overline{\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}}:{\rm dist\,}(df_{\gamma,\sigma,p},A_{1})\}\right|=(1-\lambda)|\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}|

(6.10)

\left|\{x\in\overline{\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}}:{\rm dist\,}(df_{\gamma,\sigma,p},A_{2})\}\right|=\lambda|\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}|

We collect the main properties we care about:

•

f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}(x)={\rm Aff}(x)
on
\partial\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}

•

(6.11)

|f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}-{\rm Aff}(x)|\precsim\sigma\gamma+\sigma|x-p|\quad\text{in $\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}$}

•

We also have (independent of
\gamma,\sigma\in(0,1]
)

\|\nabla f_{\gamma,\sigma,p}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p})}\precsim_{A_{1},A_{2},C,{\rm Aff}}1.

The set
\overline{\Omega_{\gamma,\sigma,p}}
is a closed set with nonempty interior, and if we set

\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\left\{\overline{\Omega_{r,\sigma,b}}\equiv p+r\overline{\Omega_{1,\sigma,0}}:\quad p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:r\in(0,1]\right\}

then
\mathcal{V}
is a regular family and a Vitali covering as in Vitali’s theorem,
Theorem

A.1
. So there exists an (at most) countable sequence
(p_{j})_{j=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
(r_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset(0,1]
such that

\overline{\Omega_{r_{j},\sigma,p_{j}}}\cap\overline{\Omega_{r_{i},\sigma,p_{i}}}=\emptyset\quad\forall i\neq j

and

(6.12)
